General representation theory in relatively closed monoidal categories
Alexey Silantyev

Abstract
We apply the notion of relative adjoint functor to generalise closed monoidal categories. We define representations in such categories and give their relation with left actions of monoids. The translation of these representations under lax monoidal functors is investigated. We introduce tensor product of representations of bimonoids as a functorial binary operation and show how symmetric lax monoidal functors act on this product. Finally we apply the general theory to classical and quantum representations.



Joint Institute for Nuclear Research, 141980 Dubna, Moscow region, Russia

State University “Dubna”, 141980 Dubna, Moscow region, Russia

Keywords:
general representation theory, relative adjoints; monoidal categories, quadratic algebras, quantum representations.

1
Introduction

The classical representation theory deals with representations of algebras, groups and other algebraic objects which can be represented by linear operators on vector spaces. An idea of quantum representations goes back to the works of Yuri Manin
[
Man87
,
Man88
,
Man91
]
, where he studied the category of finitely generated quadratic algebras. He proposed to regard the objects of the opposite category as a ‘non-commutative’ (or ‘quantum’) generalisation of finite-dimensional vector spaces and called them
quantum linear spaces
.

In
[
S21
]
we introduced a notion of quantum representation, which generalises finite-dimensional representations of finite-dimensional algebras and algebraic groups to the case of quantum linear representation space. To define and investigate quantum representations we used there a general approach to representations in monoidal categories. In the present paper we continue to develop this approach, which we call
general representation theory
.

A representation of an algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}
on a vector space
V
can be regarded in two ways: as an action
a\colon{\mathfrak{A}}\otimes V\to V
or as an algebra homomorphism
\rho\colon{\mathfrak{A}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
is the algebra of linear operators on
V
. We use the word ‘representation’ for such morphisms
\rho
. This is a basic example of the general notion of representation in a monoidal category for the category of vector spaces, where the role of the monoidal product is played by the tensor product
\otimes
.

The notion of action is generalised for any (symmetric) monoidal category
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
as an action of a monoid (we consider the case of symmetric monoidal categories only). This notion is equivalent to the notion of representation iff
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is closed. In this case we can use the internal hom-functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
to define representations and relate them with the (left) actions. In
[
S21
, § 3]
we considered more general situation, when the functor
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a right adjoint for each
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
, where
{\mathbf{P}}
is a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{C}}
. We used the theorem on adjunction with a parameter
[
MacLane
, § 4.7, Th. 3]
to obtain a generalised internal hom-functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
with a natural isomorphism

\alpha_{X,V,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X\otimes V,Z)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big)
, where
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
.

But it turned out that this condition is too strong. We need the isomorphisms
\alpha_{X,V,Z}
for
Z\in{\mathbf{P}}
only. This can not be formulated in terms of usual adjoint functors, so we use the notion of relative adjoints introduced by Friedrich Ulmer in
[
Ulm
]
.
We require that for each
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
the functor
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a right adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
and then we say that the symmetric monoidal category
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is
closed relative
to the
parametrising subcategory

{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
. This approach allows us to generalise the results of
[
S21
]
to the monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}
opposite to the category of all the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebras with the Manin product ‘
\circ
’ and the parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}
consisting of the quantum linear spaces.

In the situations such as Quantum Representation Theory it is more convenient to work in the opposite category to the category
{\mathbf{C}}
. By this reason we double all the important results for this case by using categorical duality: we consider monoidal categories coclosed relative to a full subcategory, comonoids, corepresentations etc.

The paper is organised as follows. In the section
2
we generalise the notion of (co)closed monoidal category by means of relative adjoints. Subsection
2.1
is preliminary. In Subsection
2.2
we formulate some corollaries of Yoneda Lemma. Subsection
2.3
is devoted to the theory of relative adjunctions. In Subsection
2.4
we define relatively (co)closed monoidal category and describe internal (co)hom and (co)end for this case. Section
3
is devoted to (co)representations in relatively (co)closed monoidal categories. In Subsection
3.1
we introduce (co)representations of (co)monoids, their morphisms and give their relation with (co)actions. In Subsection
3.2
we describe tensor product of (co)representations of bimonoids. The translation of the (co)representations and their tensor products under monoidal functors is investigated in Subsection
3.3
. Then, in Section
4
, we give the basic examples. Classical representations of various types of groups, algebras and their mixed (semi-linear) versions is described in Subsection
4.1
in terms of the general representation theory. In Subsection
4.2
we apply the general theory to the quantum representations.

Acknowledgements
.
The author thanks V. Rubtsov for useful advice.

2
Relative adjunctions and relatively closed monoidal categories

Before construction of the general representation theory we need to introduce some types of monoidal categories which is appropriate for definition of (co)representations. The condition on such monoidal categories is given by means of the notion of relative adjoints (with a parameter). For completeness we formulate and prove some needed statements on the relative adjoints. This requires in turn some facts following from Yoneda Lemma.

2.1
Notations and conventions

Fist of all, let us fix some conventions. In this work we use the terms and notations introduced in
[
S21
, § 2]
. Let us remind some of them. We also recall some definitions from
[
MacLane
]
and introduce some terminology used below.

2.1.1.

Morphisms and functors.
In order to unify the notations with the previous publications we denote the composition of morphisms
f,g
in a category
{\mathbf{C}}
by
f\cdot g
instead of
f\circ g
. For a fixed object
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
denote by
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,-)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
the functor from
{\mathbf{C}}
to the category of sets, it maps an object
Y\in{\mathbf{C}}
to the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}(X,Y)\in{\mathbf{Set}}
which consists of all the morphisms in
{\mathbf{C}}
of the form
X\to Y
, a morphism
f\colon Y\to Z
is translated by this functor to the map
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,f)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Z)
,
g\mapsto f\cdot g\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Z)
,
\;\forall\,g\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)
. Analogously we define the contravariant functor
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(-,X)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(f,X)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Z,X)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Y,X)
,
h\mapsto h\cdot f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Y,X)
,
h\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Z,X)
. Together these formulae give the bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(-,-)\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
.

Let
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a functor from a category
{\mathbf{C}}
to a category
{\mathbf{D}}
. If
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
is a subcategory, then we can restrict
F
to
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and get a new functor
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
, which we usually denote by the same letter
F
; formally, this is a composition
F\cdot E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}
, where
E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
is the categorical embedding. If
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
is a subcategory of
{\mathbf{D}}
such that
Ff
is a morphism in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
for any morphism
f
in
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
, then the restriction gives a functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
.

2.1.2.

Natural transformations.

Let
\alpha\colon F\to G
be a natural transformation between functors
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
and
G\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
. We sometimes omit the subscript
X
of the components
\alpha_{X}\colon FX\to GX
. For instance, for a morphism
f\colon Y\to Z
in
{\mathbf{C}}
the formulae
f_{*}(g)=f\cdot g
and
f^{*}(h)=h\cdot f
define the components
f_{*}=(f_{*})_{X}=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,f)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Z)
and
f^{*}=(f^{*})_{X}=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(f,X)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Z,X)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Y,X)
of the natural transformations
f_{*}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(-,Y)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(-,Z)
and
f^{*}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Z,-)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Y,-)
respectively.

For natural transformations
\alpha\colon F\to G
and
\beta\colon G\to H
between functors
F,G,H\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
we denote their composition by
\beta\cdot\alpha\colon F\to H
. This is a natural transformation with the components
(\beta\cdot\alpha)_{X}=\beta_{X}\cdot\alpha_{X}\colon FX\to HX
.

We say that the morphisms
\alpha=\alpha_{X}\colon FX\to GX
are natural in
X
iff they define a natural transformation
\alpha\colon F\to G
.
Recall that the natural transformation
\alpha\colon F\to G
is an isomorphism in the category of functors
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
iff all the components
\alpha=\alpha_{X}\colon FX\to GX
are isomorphisms in
{\mathbf{D}}
; the inverse natural transformation
\alpha^{-1}\colon G\to F
has components
\alpha^{-1}=(\alpha_{X})^{-1}\colon GX\to FX
inverse to
\alpha_{X}
. Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
be a subcategory, then we say that
\alpha=\alpha_{X}\colon FX\to GX
are natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
iff they define a natural transformation between the restricted functors
F
and
G
, i.e.
\alpha\colon F\cdot E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\to G\cdot E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}
. In this case it is enough to suppose that the morphisms
\alpha_{X}
are defined for
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
.

Let
{\mathbf{P}}
be also a category and
F
and
G
be bifunctors
{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
. Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{P}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{P}}
be subcategories. Then the requirement that
\alpha=\alpha_{X,Y}\colon F(X,Y)\to G(X,Y)
are natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}^{\prime}
means that these morphisms give a natural transformation between the restrictions of
F
and
G
to the subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{P}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}
. For a fixed object
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
we have functors
F_{Y},G_{Y}\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
such that
F_{Y}(f)=F(f,Y)
and
G_{Y}(f)=G(f,Y)
for any morphism
f
in
{\mathbf{C}}
. These functors can be interpreted as functors with a parameter. We say that
\alpha=\alpha_{X,Y}\colon F_{Y}(X)\to G_{Y}(X)
are natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
iff for any
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
they give a natural transformation between the restricted functors
F_{Y},G_{Y}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
(in this case we do not suppose that
F_{Y}
and
G_{Y}
are functorial in
Y
, i.e. morphisms
F(f,X)
and
G(f,X)
may be undefined). Analogous conventions are supposed for the functors with three or more arguments.

Note that any functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
can be considered as the natural transformation between the functors
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}\big(F(-),F(-)\big)\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
with the components
F\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(FX,FY)
. By fixing one of the arguments we obtain the natural transformation
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,-)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(FX,F(-)\big)
or
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(-,Y)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F(-),FY\big)
.

2.2
Corollaries of Yoneda Lemma

Yoneda Lemma and its corollaries are the main tool in the theory of adjoints and their relative version.

2.2.1.

Yoneda Lemma.

Let
R
be an object of a category
{\mathbf{D}}
and
K\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
be a functor.
Yoneda Lemma establishes a one-to-one correspondence between the natural transformations from the functor
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,-)\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
to the functor
K
and the elements of the set
KR
(see
[
MacLane
, § 3.2]
). It can be formulated as follows.

Lemma 2.1
.

Any natural transformation
\alpha\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,-)\to K
can be written in the form
\alpha_{Z}(f)=K(f)(u)\in KZ
,
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,Z)
, for a unique element
u\in KR
.

We are interested in some particular cases of this Lemma and their corollaries.

2.2.2.

The case
K=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,G(-)\big)
.

Let
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
be a functor and
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
. Consider the functor
K=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,G(-)\big)
, it maps an object
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
to the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,GZ)
and a morphism
f\colon Z\to Z^{\prime}
to the map
(Gf)_{*}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,GZ)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,GZ^{\prime})
. In this case Yoneda Lemma
2.1
takes the following form.

Corollary 2.2
.

Any maps
\alpha_{Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,Z)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,GZ)
natural in
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
have the form
\alpha(f)=(Gf)\cdot\gamma
for a unique morphism
\gamma\colon X\to GR
.

Denote the natural transformation
\alpha\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,-)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,G(-)\big)
given by a morphism
\gamma\colon X\to GR
as in Corollary
2.2
by
\alpha=\gamma^{*}\cdot G
, this is a composition of natural transformations in the sense of p.
2.1
, where the functor
G
is regarded as the natural transformation
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,-)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(GR,G(-)\big)
(this is not ‘horizontal’ composition defined in
[
MacLane
, § 2.5]
). If
{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{D}}
and
G=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{C}}}
, then
\alpha=\gamma^{*}
.

Consider a category
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and functors
F_{1}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
,
F_{2}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
.

Corollary 2.3
.

The morphisms
\gamma_{X}\colon F_{2}X\to GF_{1}X
are natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
iff the morphisms
\alpha_{X,Z}=\gamma_{X}^{*}\cdot G\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{1}X,Z)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{2}X,GZ)
are natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If
\gamma_{X}\colon F_{2}X\to GF_{1}X
are natural in
X
, then for any morphism
g\colon X\to Y
in
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
we have
\gamma_{Y}\cdot F_{2}g=GF_{1}g\cdot\gamma_{X}
, hence
(F_{2}g)^{*}\cdot\gamma_{Y}^{*}\cdot G=(\gamma_{Y}\cdot F_{2}g)^{*}\cdot G=(GF_{1}g\cdot\gamma_{X})^{*}\cdot G=\gamma_{X}^{*}\cdot G\cdot(F_{1}g)^{*}
. This implies that the diagram

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{1}Y,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\scriptstyle{(F_{1}g)^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(GF_{1}Y,GZ)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{Y}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{2}Y,GZ)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F_{2}g)^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{1}X,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(GF_{1}X,GZ)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{X}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{2}X,GZ)}

is commutative, which means exactly that
\alpha_{X,Z}
is natural in
X
. Conversely, let the diagram (
2.2
) be commutative. Denote
\delta_{Z}=\gamma_{X}^{*}\cdot G\cdot(F_{1}g)^{*}=\gamma_{X}^{*}\cdot(GF_{1}g)^{*}\cdot G
, this is the diagonal arrow
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{1}X,Z)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{2}Y,GZ)
in the diagram (
2.2
). Note that
\delta_{Z}
are natural in
Z
, so by virtue of Corollary
2.2
there exist unique morphisms
f_{X,Y}\colon F_{2}Y\to F_{1}X
such that
\delta_{Z}=f_{X,Y}^{*}\cdot G
. Hence
\gamma_{Y}\cdot F_{2}g=f_{X,Y}=GF_{1}g\cdot\gamma_{X}
. ∎

2.2.3.

Universal morphisms.

Recall that
universal morphism from an object
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
to a functor
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is a pair
(R,\eta)
, where
R\in{\mathbf{D}}
and
\eta\colon X\to GR
are an object and a morphism such that for any
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
and
f\colon X\to GZ
there is a unique
h\colon R\to Z
such that
f=Gh\cdot\eta
.

Analogously,
universal morphism from a functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
to an object
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
is a pair
(P,\varepsilon)
, where
P\in{\mathbf{C}}
and
\varepsilon\colon FP\to Z
are such that for any
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
and
h\colon FX\to Z
there is a unique
f\colon X\to P
such that
h=\varepsilon\cdot Ff
. This notion can be obtained from the previous one by the categorical duality.

In terms of p.
2.2
the condition that
(R,\eta)
is a universal morphism from an object
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
to a functor
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is equivalent to the bijectivity of the natural transformation
\eta^{*}\cdot G\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,-)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,G(-)\big)
. Due to Corollary
2.2
we can reformulate it as follows (see
[
MacLane
, § 3.2, Prop. 1]
).

Corollary 2.4
.

Any bijections
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,Z)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,GZ\big)
natural in
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
have the form
\eta^{*}\cdot G
for a unique universal morphism
(R,\eta)
from
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
to
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
.

Corollary 2.5
.

Consider functors
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
,
F_{1}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
and
F_{2}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
. Any bijections
\beta_{X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{1}X,Z)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F_{2}X,GZ\big)
natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
have the form
\eta_{X}^{*}\cdot G
for a unique natural transformation
\eta\colon F_{2}\to GF_{1}
such that
(F_{1}X,\eta_{X})
is a universal morphism from
F_{2}X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
to
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
.

Proof.
This statement follows directly from Corollaries
2.4
and
2.3
. ∎

2.2.4.

The case
K=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,-)
.

If the functor
K
has the form
K=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,-)
for some
X\in{\mathbf{D}}
, then Yoneda Lemma
2.1
is a particular case of Corollary
2.2
for
{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{D}}
and
G=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{D}}}
. It can be written in the following form (see
[
MacLane
, § 3.2]
).

Corollary 2.6
.

Any natural transformation
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,-)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,-)
has the form
\gamma^{*}
for a unique morphism
\gamma\colon X\to R
.

Note that
(R,\gamma)
is a universal morphism form
R
to
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}
iff
\gamma
is an isomorphism. Hence Corollary
2.4
takes the following from.

Corollary 2.7
.

The natural transformation
\gamma^{*}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,-)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,-)
is an isomorphism of functors iff
\gamma\colon X\to R
is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Remark 2.8
.

The universal morphism is unique up to an isomorphism: if
(R,\eta)
and
(R^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
are two universal morphisms from
X
to
G
, then there is a unique isomorphism
\gamma\colon R\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}R^{\prime}
such that
G\gamma\cdot\eta=\eta^{\prime}
. This fact is deduced in
[
MacLane
, § 3.1]
by using comma categories, however it also follows from (the corollaries of) Yoneda Lemma. To show it one needs to apply Corollaries
2.6
and
2.7
to the composition of
(\eta^{\prime})^{*}\cdot G\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R^{\prime},-)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,G(-)\big)
with the inverse of
\eta^{*}\cdot G\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(R,-)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,G(-)\big)
and then to take into account Corollary
2.2
. Dually, a universal morphism
(P,\varepsilon)
from
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
to an object
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
is unique up to a unique isomorphism.

Let
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
be functors
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
. Corollaries
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.5
imply the following statements.

Corollary 2.9
.

If maps
\beta_{X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{1}X,Z)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{2}X,Z)
are natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
, then
\beta_{X,Z}=\gamma_{X}^{*}
for a unique natural transformation
\gamma\colon F_{2}\to F_{1}
.

Corollary 2.10
.

Any bijections
\beta_{X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{1}X,Z)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(F_{2}X,Z)
natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
have the form
\beta_{X,Z}=\gamma_{X}^{*}
for a unique natural isomorphism
\gamma\colon F_{2}\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}F_{1}
.

2.3
Relative right and left adjoint functors

Now we introduce a notion of relative right/left adjunctions and its version ‘with a parameter’ which are necessary for an appropriate generalisation of the (co)closed monoidal categories.

2.3.1.

Relative adjunctions.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
be categories. Recall that a functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is called
left adjoint
for
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
and
G
is called
right adjoint
for
F
when there exist bijections

\displaystyle\alpha_{X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}(FX,Z)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}(X,GZ)

(2.5)

natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
. It gives a natural isomorphism
\alpha
between the bifunctors defined as compositions

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}\big(F(-),-\big)\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{F^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}}{\mathbf{Set}},

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}\big(-,G(-)\big)\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\times G}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}}{\mathbf{Set}},

(2.7)

where
F^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the functor opposite to
F
. The isomorphism
\alpha
is called
adjunction bijection
for the adjoints
F,G
. The triple
(F,G,\alpha)
is called
adjunction

[
MacLane
, § 4.1]
.

Consider a full subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
. If we restrict the functors (
2.6
), (
2.7
) to the subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}
, then we obtain isomorphisms (
2.5
) natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
. In this case it is sufficient to define the functor
G
on the subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
, however the functors
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
and
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
are not adjoint to each other, because the domain of such
G
does not coincide with the codomain of
F
(we can not reduce this situation to the adjunction case unless
F({\mathbf{C}})\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
, when one can consider
F
as a functor
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
).

A generalisation of the notion of adjoint functor appropriate for this situation was introduced by Ulmer in
[
Ulm
, Def. 2.3]
. Namely, let
{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{D}}
be categories and
J\colon{\mathbf{B}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
,
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
,
G\colon{\mathbf{B}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
be functors. A functor
G
is called
right adjoint for
F
relative to
J
iff
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}\big(F(-),J(-)\big)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}\big(-,G(-)\big)
are isomorphic as functors
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{B}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
. Similarly relative left adjoint is defined
[
Ulm
, Def. 2.2]
. In this work we consider the case of fully faithful functor
J
only. For this case we can interpret
{\mathbf{B}}
as a subcategory of
{\mathbf{D}}
and
J
as the corresponding category embedding (up to category equivalence the notion of subcategory essentially coincides with the notion of fully faithful functor). Let us formulate the definition of relative right/left adjoint functor for this particular case in details.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a functor. A functor
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is called
right adjoint for the functor
F
relative to
the full subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
iff there exist isomorphisms of the form (
2.5
) natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
. These isomorphisms give a natural isomorphism
\alpha
between the functors

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{F^{\mathrm{op}}\times E_{{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}}}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}}{\mathbf{Set}},

(2.8)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\times G}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}}{\mathbf{Set}};

(2.9)

then
(F,G,\alpha)
is called
relative right adjunction
and
\alpha
is called
right adjunction bijection
. In this case we say that
F

has a right adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
.

Let
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
be a functor. A functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is called
left adjoint for the functor
G
relative to
the full subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
iff there exist isomorphisms of the form (
2.5
) natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
. These isomorphisms give a natural isomorphism
\alpha
between the functors

\displaystyle({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{F^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}}{\mathbf{Set}},

(2.10)

\displaystyle({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{E_{({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}}\times G}{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}}{\mathbf{Set}};

(2.11)

then
(F,G,\alpha)
is called
relative left adjunction
and
\alpha
is called
left adjunction bijection
. In this case we say that
G

has a left adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
.

Note that
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is a right adjoint for
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
relative to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
iff the opposite functor
G^{\mathrm{op}}\colon({\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a left adjoint for
F^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}
relative to
({\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Warning 2.12
.

In contrast to the case of usual adjunction the fact the
F
is a relative left adjoint for
G
does not mean that
G
is the relative right adjoint for
F
and vice versa.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime}
be a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
. We see from the definition that if
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is a right adjoint for
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
relative to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
, then the restriction of
G
to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime}
is a right adjoint for
F
relative to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime}
. In particular, if
F
has a usual right adjoint (i.e. relative to the whole
{\mathbf{D}}
), then it has a right adjoint relative to any subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
. The same we can say about relative left adjoint functors.

Sometimes a right/left adjoint for a fixed functor does not exists, but there exists a right/left adjoint relative to some full subcategory. Below we show how to determine the maximal full subcategory such that a right/left adjoint exists relative to this subcategory.

A typical example is the forgetful functor
G\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
from the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces to the category of sets: the usual left adjoint does not exists, but we have a left adjoint relative to the subcategory of the finite sets.

In applications we sometimes have situations, when we are not interested if a usual right/left adjoint exists, we only need to establish the existence of the right/left adjoint relative to a fixed full subcategory.

2.3.2.

Units and counits for relative adjunctions.

Let us generalise some properties and ‘equivalent definitions’ of the adjoint functors (
[
MacLane
, § 4.1, Theorems 1, 2]
) for the case of relative adjoints. In some form they were formulated in
[
Ulm
]
without proof. For completeness we briefly add the proofs by following ideas of
[
MacLane
]
and by applying the corollaries of Yoneda Lemma.

Theorem 2.13
.

Consider a functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
and a full subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
.

(1).

Let
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
be a right adjoint for
F
relative to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
. The right adjunction bijections
\alpha_{X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(FX,Z)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,GZ)
are inverse to

\displaystyle\alpha^{-1}(f)=\varepsilon_{Z}\cdot Ff,

\displaystyle f\colon X\to GZ,

\displaystyle X\in{\mathbf{C}},

\displaystyle Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime},

(2.12)

where
\varepsilon_{Z}:=\alpha^{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{GZ})\colon FGZ\to Z
are natural in
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
. The pair
(GZ,\varepsilon_{Z})
is a universal morphism from
F
to
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
.

(2).

Suppose that for any
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
there exists a universal morphism
(X_{Z},\varepsilon_{Z})
from
F
to
Z
. Then
F
has a right adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
. This relative right adjoint can be defined as the unique functor
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
such that
GZ=X_{Z}

\;\forall\,Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon_{Z}
form a natural transformation from
FG
to
E_{{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}}
. The corresponding right adjunction bijections can be written via its inverse by the formula (
2.12
).

Dually, consider a functor
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
and a full subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
.

(1^{\prime}).

Let
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a left adjoint for
G
relative to
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
. The left adjunction bijections
\alpha_{X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(FX,Z)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,GZ)
can be written as

\displaystyle\alpha(h)=Gh\cdot\eta_{X},

\displaystyle h\colon FX\to Z,

\displaystyle X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},

\displaystyle Z\in{\mathbf{D}},

(2.13)

where
\eta_{X}:=\alpha(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FX})\colon X\to GFX
are natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
, and
(FX,\eta_{X})
is a universal morphism from
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
to
G
.

(2^{\prime}).

Suppose that for any
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
there exists a universal morphism
(Z_{X},\eta_{X})
from
X
to
G
. Then
G
has a left adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. This relative left adjoint can be defined as the unique functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
such that
FX=Z_{X}

\;\forall\,X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
\eta_{X}
form a natural transformation from
E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}
to
GF
. The corresponding left adjunction bijections have the form (
2.13
).

Proof.
The statements
(1)
and
(2)
are equivalent to
(1^{\prime})
and
(2^{\prime})
via the categorical duality, so it is sufficient to prove the latter two.

(1^{\prime})
: This is a particular case of Corollary
2.5
for
F_{1}=F
and
F_{2}=E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}
.

(2^{\prime})
: The functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is constructed as follows. Define it on objects
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
as
FX=Z_{X}
. For a morphism
g\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
we define
Fg
as the unique morphism
FX\to FX^{\prime}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{GFX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{GFg}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{GFX^{\prime}}

is commutative. By construction the functor
F
is a unique functor
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
such that
FX=Z_{X}

\;\forall\,X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and the morphisms
\eta_{X}\colon X\to GFX
are natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. Define the maps
\alpha_{X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(FX,Z)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,GZ)
by the formula (
2.13
), which implies that
\alpha_{X,Z}
are natural in
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
. Their naturality in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
follows from the naturality of
\eta
. The bijectivity of the map
\alpha_{X,Z}
follows from the universality of
(FX,\eta_{X})
, so
F
is a left adjoint for
G
relative to
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. ∎

The natural transformation
\eta\colon E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\to GF
with the components
\eta_{X}:=\alpha_{X,FX}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FX})
is called
unit
for the relative left adjunction
(F,G,\alpha)
. Dually, the natural transformation
\varepsilon\colon FG\to E_{{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}}
defined in Theorem
2.13
is called
counit
for the relative right adjunction
(F,G,\alpha)
. Note that in general we do not have a unit for a relative right adjunction, nor a counit for a relative left adjunction.

We see from Theorem
2.13
that the maximal full subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
such that the functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
has a right adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
can be found as follows. It consists of all objects
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
such that there is a universal morphism from
F
to
Z
. Analogously, the existence of a universal morphism from
X
to
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
defines the maximal full subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
such that
G
has a left adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
.

2.3.3.

Relative adjunctions with a parameter.

For a bifunctor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
we can consider a family of functors
F_{Y}=F(-,Y)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
parametrised by objects of the category
{\mathbf{P}}
. If
F_{Y}
has a right adjoint
G_{Y}
for any
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
, then there is a unique functor
G\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
such that
G(Y,f)=G_{Y}(f)
for any morphism
f
in
{\mathbf{D}}
and the corresponding adjunction bijections

\displaystyle\alpha_{X,Y,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}\big(F(X,Y),Z\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}\big(X,G(Y,Z)\big)

(2.18)

are natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
(see
[
MacLane
, § 4.7, Th. 3]
). This fact is immediately generalised to the case of relative adjunctions.

Theorem 2.14
.

Let
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a bifunctor such that the functor
F(-,Y)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
has a right adjoint relative to a full subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
for each
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
, so we have a relative right adjunction
\big(F(-,Y),G_{Y},\alpha_{Y}\big)
for each
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
, where
G_{Y}\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is the relative right adjoint and
\alpha_{Y}
is the corresponding right adjunction bijection. Then there exists a unique functor
G\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
such that
G(Y,-)=G_{Y}
and the components
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}=(\alpha_{Y})_{X,Z}
give the isomorphism of the form (
2.18
) natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
.

Let
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
be a bifunctor such that each functor
G(-,Y)\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a left adjoint relative to a full subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
, so we have a relative left adjunction
\big(F_{Y},G(-,Y),\alpha_{Y}\big)
for each
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
, where
F_{Y}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
. Then there exists a unique functor
F\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
such that
F(Y,-)=F_{Y}
and the components
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}=(\alpha_{Y})_{X,Z}
give the isomorphism of the form

\displaystyle\alpha_{X,Y,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{D}}\big(F(Y,X),Z\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}\big(X,G(Z,Y)\big)

(2.19)

natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
.

Proof.
Let us prove the second part (the first part is dual). By having the functors
F_{Y}=F(Y,-)\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
for
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
we need to construct a bifunctor
F\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
such that (
2.19
) is natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
. This amounts to construction of functors
\widetilde{F}_{X}=F(-,X)\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
satisfying the following conditions:
F_{Y}(X)=\widetilde{F}_{X}(Y)
, the bijection (
2.19
) is natural in
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
(for each fixed
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
) and the diagram

\textstyle{F(Y^{\prime},X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(Y^{\prime},f)}
\scriptstyle{F(g,X)}
\textstyle{F(Y^{\prime},X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(g,X^{\prime})}
\textstyle{F(Y,X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(Y,f)}
\textstyle{F(Y,X^{\prime})}

commutes for any morphisms
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
g\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{P}}
(see
[
MacLane
, § 2.3, Prop. 1, 2]
). For each such
g\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
consider the morphism
\beta_{X,Z}(g)
defined by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F(Y,X),Z\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{X,Y,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X,Z}(g)}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,G(Z,Y)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G(Z,g)_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F(Y^{\prime},X),Z\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{X,Y^{\prime},Z}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,G(Z,Y^{\prime})\big)}

It is natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
, since
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}
is so. Hence by virtue of Corollary
2.9
there exist unique morphisms
F(g,X)\colon F(Y^{\prime},X)\to F(Y,X)
natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
such that
\beta_{X,Z}(g)=F(g,X)^{*}
for any
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
. Note that
\beta_{X,Z}(g_{1}\cdot g_{2})=\beta_{X,Z}(g_{1})\cdot\beta_{X,Z}(g_{2})
for any morphisms
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
in
{\mathbf{P}}
, so the uniqueness of
F(g,X)
implies that
\widetilde{F}_{X}=F(-,X)
is a functor
{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
for each
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. We see from the diagram (
2.3
) that it makes
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}
to be natural in
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
. The uniqueness of the morphisms implies also that such functor is unique. The naturality of
F(g,X)
in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
means exactly the commutativity of the diagram (
2.3
). ∎

Definition 2.15
.

A
right adjunction with a parameter
for a bifunctor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{D}}

relative
to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
is a triple
(F,G,\alpha)
, where
G\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is a functor and
\alpha
is an isomorphism of the form (
2.18
) natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
.
A
left adjunction with a parameter
for a bifunctor
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}

relative
to
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
is a triple
(F,G,\alpha)
, where
F\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a functor and
\alpha
is an isomorphism of the form (
2.19
) natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
.

Theorem
2.14
implies the following fact: a right/left adjunction with a parameter for a bifunctor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
relative to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
exists iff the functor
F(-,Y)
has a right/left adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
for each object
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
.

The counits of the relative right adjunctions
\big(F(-,Y),G(Y,-),\alpha\big)
have components

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{Y,Z}=\alpha^{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{G(Y,Z)})\colon F\big(G(Y,Z),Y\big)\to Z.

(2.28)

These are morphisms in
{\mathbf{D}}
natural in
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
. They are dinatural in
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
in the sense of
[
MacLane
, § 9.4]
.

The units of the relative left adjunctions
\big(F(Y,-),G(-,Y),\alpha\big)
have components

\displaystyle\eta_{Y,X}=\alpha(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{F(Y,X)})\colon X\to G\big(F(Y,X),Y\big).

(2.29)

These are morphisms in
{\mathbf{C}}
natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and dinatural in
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
.

Note that relative right/left adjunction is a particular case of the relative right/left adjunction with a parameter. This is the case of the category
{\mathbf{P}}
which has a unique object and a unique arrow.

2.3.4.

Uniqueness of relative adjunctions.

If a usual right/left adjoint to a fixed functor exists, then it is unique up to an isomorphism
[
MacLane
, § 4.1, Corollary 1]
. Moreover, this isomorphism is unique and it relates the corresponding adjunction bijections. The same still holds for the case of relative adjunctions. We prove this for more general case: for the relative adjunctions with a parameter.

Theorem 2.16
.

If there exist two right adjunctions with a parameter
(F,G,\alpha)
and
(F,G^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
for
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
relative to a full subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
, then there exists a unique natural isomorphism
\gamma\colon G\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}G^{\prime}
such that
(\gamma_{Y,Z})_{*}\cdot\alpha_{X,Y,Z}=\alpha^{\prime}_{X,Y,Z}
for all
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}
.

Dually, if there exist two left adjunctions with a parameter
(F,G,\alpha)
and
(F^{\prime},G,\alpha^{\prime})
for
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
relative to a full subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
, then there exists a unique natural isomorphism
\gamma\colon F\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}F^{\prime}
such that
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}\cdot(\gamma_{Y,X})^{*}=\alpha^{\prime}_{X,Y,Z}
for all
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
.

Proof.
As above we prove the ‘left’ version.
Consider the compositions

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F^{\prime}(Y,X),Z\big)\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}_{X,Y,Z}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,G(Z,Y)\big)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{X,Y,Z}^{-1}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F(Y,X),Z\big).

(2.30)

These are bijections
\beta_{Y,X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F^{\prime}(Y,X),Z\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F(Y,X),Z\big)

natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{D}}
. By virtue of Corollary
2.10
they have the form
\beta_{Y,X,Z}=\gamma_{Y,X}^{*}
for a unique natural isomorphism
\gamma\colon F\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}F^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 2.17
.

The uniqueness property of relative adjunctions (with a parameter) can be rewritten in terms of counits (
2.28
) and units (
2.29
) respectively. Let
\eta_{Y,X}=\alpha(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{F(Y,X)})
and
\eta_{Y,X}=\alpha^{\prime}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{F^{\prime}(Y,X)})
be units for the relative left adjunctions with a parameter
(F,G,\alpha)
and
(F^{\prime},G,\alpha^{\prime})
respectively. The natural isomorphism
\gamma\colon F\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}F^{\prime}
from Theorem
2.16
relates these units by the formula
\eta^{\prime}_{Y,X}=G(\gamma_{Y,X},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Y})\cdot\eta_{Y,X}
. The existence and uniqueness of such isomorphisms
\gamma_{Y,X}\colon F(Y,X)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}F^{\prime}(Y,X)
follows from the fact that a universal morphism from
X
to
G(-,Y)
is unique up to a unique isomorphism (see Remark
2.8
). Analogously, the counits
\varepsilon_{Y,Z}=\alpha^{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{G(Y,Z)})
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{Y,Z}=(\alpha^{\prime})^{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{G^{\prime}(Y,Z)})
are related as
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{Y,Z}\cdot F(\gamma_{Z,Y},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Y})=\varepsilon_{Y,Z}
for unique
\gamma_{Z,Y}
.

2.4
Relatively closed monoidal categories

Representations can be defined in a closed monoidal category. But sometimes we need to consider more general situation. The notion of adjunction (with a parameter) relative to a full subcategory allows us to generalise closed monoidal category to the case sufficient for our purposes.

2.4.1.

Generalisations of closed and coclosed monoidal categories.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
be a symmetric monoidal category with a monoidal product
-\otimes-\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
.
As in
[
S21
]
we suppose that the monoidal categories are strict. We denote the unit object of
{\mathbf{C}}
by
I_{\mathbf{C}}
. Recall that
{\mathbf{C}}
is called
closed monoidal category
iff the functor
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a right adjoint for each
V\in{\mathbf{C}}
. This right adjoint is denoted as
h\mapsto\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,h)
and gives a bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
called
internal hom-functor
. Since
{\mathbf{C}}
is symmetric, the right adjoint functors for
-\otimes V
and
V\otimes-
coincide with each other. Dually,
{\mathbf{C}}
is called
coclosed monoidal category
iff
-\otimes V
has a left adjoint for each
V\in{\mathbf{C}}
. In this case we obtain
internal cohom-functor

\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
.

For purposes of Quantum Representation Theory we considered more general situation in
[
S21
]
, when the functors
-\otimes V
have right/left adjoints for the objects
V
of a full subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
(we do not always suppose that this subcategory is monoidal). For this situation we introduced representations/corepresentations of a monoid/comonoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
on an object
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
. However the condition for these functors to have adjoints is too strong. Here we consider a weaker condition by requiring the existence of right/left adjoints relative to
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
.

Definition 2.18
.

The symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is called
closed/coclosed relative to
a full subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
or
relatively closed/coclosed with a parametrising subcategory

{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
iff the functor
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a right/left adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
for each object
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
.

The following facts are proved by restricting the corresponding relative adjoint functors.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
be a symmetric monoidal category and
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
be its full subcategory.

(1).

If the monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}
is closed/coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
, then it is closed/coclosed relative to any full subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{P}}
.

(2).

Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
be a full subcategory such that
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. If the functor
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a right/left adjoint relative to
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
for each
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
, then
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is closed/coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
.

(3).

In particular, if the functor
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a right/left adjoint for each
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
, then the monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}
is closed/coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
.

The condition supposed in the point (3) of Prop.
2.19
is often fulfilled, however this stronger condition is not needed to construct the representation theory. The subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
is usually chosen as bigger as possible such that the functor
-\otimes V
has a right/left (relative) adjoint for all
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
. In some cases it is easier to check that these functors have adjoints relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
than relative to the whole
{\mathbf{C}}
.

The simplest non-trivial example is the category of vector spaces
{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{Vect}}
with the standard tensor product
\otimes
, where the parametrising subcategory is
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FVect}}
. The functor
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
has a left adjoint for any
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
(see
[
S21
, § 3.1.11]
), so due to the point (3) of Prop.
2.19
the symmetric monoidal category
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{FVect}}
.

2.4.2.

Internal hom.

Due to Theorem
2.14
(for the case
{\mathbf{D}}={\mathbf{C}}
) a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is relatively closed with the parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
iff there exists a bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
with the bijections

\displaystyle\theta=\theta_{X,V,W}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X\otimes V,W)

(2.31)

natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Or, equivalently, iff there exists a right adjunction with a parameter
(-\otimes-,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits,\theta^{-1})
relative to the full subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{P}}
. The bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits
is called
(relative) internal hom-functor
. The values
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
on the objects
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
are called
internal hom-objects
.

One can consider the bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits
with the natural isomorphism
\theta
as an additional structure on the symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}
. However,
Theorem
2.16
implies that the relative right adjunction with a parameter
\big(-\otimes-,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits,\theta^{-1}\big)
is unique up to a unique natural isomorphism. This means that the choice of the certain pair
(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits,\theta)
is not essential, all the choices are equivalent.

Dually, a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is relatively coclosed with the parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
iff there exists a bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
, called
(relative) internal cohom-functor
, with the bijections

\displaystyle\vartheta=\vartheta_{W,V,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W),Z\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,Z\otimes V)

(2.32)

natural in
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
. This means that
(-\otimes-,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits,\vartheta)
is a left adjunction with a parameter relative to the full subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{P}}
; it is also unique up to an isomorphism. The values
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)
on the objects
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
are called
internal cohom-objects
.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
be a symmetric monoidal category and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
be its full monoidal subcategory. Consider also a full subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
.

(1).

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}

\;\forall\,V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
, then
({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},\otimes)
is also closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
. The internal hom-functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
equals to the composition of
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
with the embedding
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
.

(2).

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}

\;\forall\,V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
, then
({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},\otimes)
is also coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
. The bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
equals to the composition of
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
with
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
.

Proof.
The condition of the point (1) imply that the bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has the form
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits=E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\cdot G
for some
G\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. For any
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
the functor
G(V,-)\colon{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
is a right adjoint for
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
. ∎

2.4.3.

Composition morphisms.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
be a relatively closed monoidal category with the parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}
. Counits (
2.28
) for
(-\otimes-,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits,\theta^{-1})
are morphisms

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}=\theta(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)})\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes V\to W,

\displaystyle V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}.

(2.33)

called
evaluations
.
The formula (
2.12
) takes the form

\displaystyle\theta_{X,V,W}(f)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\cdot(f\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}),

\displaystyle X\in{\mathbf{C}},\;\;V,W\in{\mathbf{P}},\quad f\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W).

(2.34)

This implies the formula

\displaystyle\theta_{\widetilde{X},V,W}(f\cdot g)=\theta_{X,V,W}(f)\cdot(g\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}),

(2.35)

where
X,\widetilde{X}\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
f\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
,
g\colon\widetilde{X}\to X
are morphisms in
{\mathbf{C}}
(see
[
S21
, Prop. 3.1]
).

For any closed monoidal category one can define internal compositions (see
[
Bor2
, § 6.1]
). Let us generalise them for the relative case. Consider the morphisms

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(U,V)\otimes U\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U,V}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes V\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}}W,

(2.36)

where
U,V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
.
By applying the map
\theta^{-1}_{X,U,W}
, where
X=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(U,V)
, to the morphism (
2.36
) we obtain

\displaystyle c_{U,V,W}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(U,V)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(U,W).

(2.37)

It is called
(internal) composition morphism
. It is the unique morphism of the form (
2.37
) such that the diagram

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(U,V)\otimes U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U,V}}
\scriptstyle{c_{U,V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(U,W)\otimes U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U,W}}
\textstyle{W}

is commutative.

In the case of relatively coclosed monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
with parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}
we have units (
2.29
) of the form

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,W}=\vartheta(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)})\colon W\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes V,

\displaystyle V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}.

(2.42)

They are called
coevaluations
.
We get the formula

\displaystyle\vartheta(f)=(f\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,W},

\displaystyle f\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\to Z.

(2.43)

The
(internal) cocomposition morphism
is

\displaystyle d_{W,V,U}=\vartheta^{-1}\big((\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{U,V})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\big)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(U,W)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(U,V),

(2.44)

where
U,V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
. This is a unique morphism making the following diagram commutative:

\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\hskip 16.38895pt}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{U,W}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{U,V}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(U,W)\otimes U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{W,V,U}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(U,V)\otimes U}

Proposition 2.21
.

The composition (
2.37
) is associative: the diagram

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(U,V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(T,U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895ptc_{U,V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes c_{T,U,V}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(U,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(T,U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{T,U,W}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(T,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895ptc_{T,V,W}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(T,W)}

commutes for any
T,U,V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Dually, cocomposition (
2.44
) is coassociative in the sense of commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(T,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{W,U,T}\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{d_{W,V,T}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(U,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(T,U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{W,V,U}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(T,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes d_{V,U,T}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(U,V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(T,U)}

Proof.
By drawing the commutative diagram (
2.4
) for each composition morphism in (
2.21
) one yields

\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{T,W}\cdot(c_{T,U,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{T})\cdot(c_{U,V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{T})=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U,V})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{T,U})=\\
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{T,W}\cdot(c_{T,V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{T})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes c_{T,U,V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{T}).

This implies commutativity of the diagram (
2.21
) due to the formula (
2.34
) and bijectivity of
\theta
(or, equivalently, due to the universality of
\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(T,W),\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{T,W}\big)
). The coassociativity of the cocomposition then follows from the duality principle. ∎

2.4.4.

Internal end.

Consider the bijection (
2.31
) with the unit object
X=I_{\mathbf{C}}
and
W=V
. Denote the image of the identification isomorphism
I\otimes V=V
under
\theta^{-1}
by

\displaystyle u_{V}:=\theta^{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})\colon I_{\mathbf{C}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V),

\displaystyle V\in{\mathbf{P}}.

(2.53)

This is a unique morphism making the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,V}}
\textstyle{V}

Lemma 2.22
.

For any
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
we have the commutative diagrams

\textstyle{I\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes u_{V}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{V,W,W}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{V,V,W}}

Proof.
The commutativity of these diagrams can be shown by means of the formula (
2.35
). Let us consider the right diagram (
2.22
). By applying the bijection
\theta
one yields

\theta\big(c_{V,V,W}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes u_{V})\big)=\theta(c_{V,V,W})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes u_{V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,V})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes u_{V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\\
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\theta(u_{V})\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\big)=\theta(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits).

The commutativity of the left diagram is proved similarly. ∎

Denote
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)
. Proposition
2.21
for the case
U=V=W
and Lemma
2.22
for
V=W
imply that the morphisms
c_{V}:=c_{V,V,V}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
and
u_{V}\colon I_{\mathbf{C}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
give a structure of monoid in
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
on the object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\in{\mathbf{C}}
.

Dually, by applying the inverse of the bijection (
2.32
), where
W=V
and
Z=I_{\mathbf{C}}
, to the identification isomorphism
V=I\otimes V
we obtain the morphism

\displaystyle v_{V}:=\vartheta^{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\to I_{\mathbf{C}},

\displaystyle V\in{\mathbf{P}}.

(2.62)

This is a unique morphism making the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,V}\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}}
\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes V}

A symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
iff its opposite category
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}=({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The hom-object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
for
{\mathbf{C}}
is the cohom-object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)
for
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The morphisms
\theta_{X,V,W}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}
for
{\mathbf{C}}
are
\vartheta_{W,V,X}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,W}
for
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The composition
c_{U,V,W}
and morphism
u_{V}
for
{\mathbf{C}}
are the cocomposition
d_{W,V,U}
and morphism
v_{V}
for
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. By taking into account this duality and argument mentioned above one can state the following.

Proposition 2.23
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is relatively closed monoidal category with parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
, then for each
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
we have the monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V),c_{V},u_{V}\big)
in
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
, where
c_{V}:=c_{V,V,V}
and
u_{V}
is (
2.53
).

Dually, if
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is relatively coclosed monoidal category with parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
, then for each
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
the morphisms
d_{V}:=d_{V,V,V}
and (
2.62
) equip the object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V):=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)
with a structure of comonoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V),d_{V},v_{V}\big)
in
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
.

The diagrams (
2.4
) for
U=V=W
and (
2.4
) imply that
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,V}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes V\to V
is a (left) action of the monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
on
V
. It also follows from the diagrams (
2.4
) for
U=V=W
and (
2.4
) that
\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,V}\colon V\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\otimes V
is a (left) coaction of the comonoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)
on
V
.

2.4.5.

The hom-functor on morphisms.
The hom-functor as well as cohom-functor is a bifunctor contravariant in the first and covariant in the second argument, i.e. a functor
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
. Let us calculate their values on morphisms in terms of evaluation and coevaluation.

Proposition 2.24
.

Let
f\colon V^{\prime}\to V
and
g\colon W\to W^{\prime}
be morphisms in
{\mathbf{P}}
. In the relatively closed case we have

\displaystyle\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)\big)=g\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)}\otimes f).

(2.67)

For the relatively coclosed case the following formula holds:

\displaystyle\vartheta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)\big)=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})}\otimes f)\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\cdot g.

(2.68)

Proof.
Repeats the proof of
[
S21
, Prop. 3.4]
. ∎

2.4.6.

The case
V=I_{\mathbf{C}}
.
Suppose that
I=I_{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Then we can define the hom-objects
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I,W)\in{\mathbf{C}}
and the monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
(in the relatively closed case) or the cohom-objects
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(I,W)\in{\mathbf{C}}
and the comonoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(I)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
(in the relatively coclosed case). We can describe them as follows (see
[
Bor2
, Prop. 6.1.8]
for the usual closed case).

Proposition 2.25
.

The object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I,W)
is naturally isomorphic to
W
via the isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I,W)=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I,W)\otimes I\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{I,W}}W.

(2.69)

In particular,
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{I,I}
gives the identification of objects
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I)=I
. As a monoid it coincides with
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I)=(I,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I})
.
The object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(I,W)
is naturally isomorphic to
W
via

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(I,W)=W\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{I,W}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(I,W)\otimes I.

(2.70)

In particular,
\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{I,I}
gives the identification
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(I)=I
. As a comonoid it has the form
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(I)=(I,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I})
.

Proof.
We prove it for the relatively coclosed case. The morphisms (
2.70
) are natural in
W\in{\mathbf{P}}
, so we need to prove that they are isomorphisms. Due to the formula (
2.43
) the natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\beta_{W,Z}=\vartheta_{W,I,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(I,W),Z\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,Z\otimes I)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,Z)

(2.71)

has the form
\beta_{W,Z}=(\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{I,W})^{*}
. Then, Corollary
2.7
implies that the coevaluations
\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{I,W}
are all isomorphisms.
To show that the last sentence of Prop.
2.25
is also valid we note that all the arrows in the diagrams (
2.4
) and (
2.4
) for
U=V=W=I
are identifications
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I}
. This implies that
d_{I}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I}
and
v_{I}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I}
. ∎

2.4.7.

The case of coreflective relatively coclosed monoidal subcategory.

Recall that a subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
is called
coreflective
iff the inclusion
E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
has a right adjoint
G\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
(see
[
MacLane
, § 4.3]
). Due to Theorem
2.13
this condition means exactly that for any
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
there exists a universal arrow
(GX,\varepsilon_{X})
from
E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}
to
X
, where the universality means that for any object
X^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and morphism
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
in
{\mathbf{C}}
there is a unique
h\colon X^{\prime}\to GX
such that the diagram

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{GX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}}
\textstyle{X}

is commutative.
We choose the adjunction such that
GX^{\prime}=X^{\prime}
,
\eta_{X^{\prime}}=\varepsilon_{X^{\prime}}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X^{\prime}}

\;\forall\,X^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Since
\varepsilon_{X}
is natural in
X
, this choice implies that the morphisms in (
2.4
) are related as
h=Gf
.

Suppose that the subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
is monoidal with respect to
\otimes
. In particular, we have
GX\otimes GY\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
for any objects
X,Y\in{\mathbf{C}}
. The commutative diagram (
2.4
) for
f=\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\varepsilon_{Y}\colon GX\otimes GY\to X\otimes Y
takes the form

\textstyle{GX\otimes GY\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\varepsilon_{Y}}
\textstyle{G(X\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X\otimes Y}}
\textstyle{X\otimes Y}

where
\phi_{X,Y}=G(\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\varepsilon_{Y})
(this is lax monoidal structure morphisms of the right adjoint
G
). The following fact will be needed in Subsection
4.2
.

Theorem 2.26
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
be a coreflective monoidal subcategory of
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and let
({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},\otimes)
be coclosed relative to a full subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. Suppose that
\phi_{X,W}\colon GX\otimes W\to G(X\otimes W)
is an isomorphism for any
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
and
W\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Then
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is also coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
. The internal cohom-functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is the composition of
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
with the embedding
E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
.

Proof.
Let
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
. The isomorphism
GX\otimes W\cong G(X\otimes W)
, universality of
\big(G(X\otimes W),\varepsilon_{X\otimes W}\big)
and commutativity of the diagram (
2.4
) for
Y=W
imply that for any
f\colon V\to X\otimes W
there exists a unique
f^{\prime}\colon V\to GX\otimes W
such that
(\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})\cdot f^{\prime}=f
. Since
GX\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
, there is a unique
h^{\prime}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,V)\to GX
such that
(h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{W,V}=f^{\prime}
, so we have the commutative diagrams

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{W,V}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\otimes W}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,V)\otimes W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pth^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W}}
\textstyle{GX\otimes W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W}}

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{W,V}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\otimes W}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,V)\otimes W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pth\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W}}

where
h=\varepsilon_{X}\cdot h^{\prime}
. Thus we proved that for any
f\colon V\to X\otimes W
there exists a morphism
h\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,V)\to X
making the second diagram commute. Now we only need to prove that it is unique. Let
g\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,V)\to X
be such that
(g\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{W,V}=f
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,V)\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
, it factors as
g=\varepsilon_{X}\cdot g^{\prime}
for some
g^{\prime}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,V)\to GX
, so we obtain
(\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})\cdot(g^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{W,V}=f=(\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})\cdot f^{\prime}
. Due to the uniqueness of
f^{\prime}
we derive
(g^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{W,V}=f^{\prime}=(h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{W,V}
. By taking into account the uniqueness of
h^{\prime}
we get
g^{\prime}=h^{\prime}
and, hence,
g=h
. ∎

Remark 2.27
.

By the duality principle one can state an analogous theorem for a reflective monoidal subcategory
({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},\otimes)\subset({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
. In this case the inclusion functor
