E_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\colon{\mathbf{P}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
has a left adjoint functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{P}}
, which is colax monoidal. If
\phi_{X,W}\colon F(X\otimes W)\to FX\otimes FW
are isomorphisms for all
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
and
W\in{\mathbf{P}}
, then
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is also closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
.

Remark 2.28
.

Note that not every symmetric monoidal category is (co)closed relative to a (co)closed monoidal subcategory. Consider a counterexample. Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be the partial order of subsets
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}
with respect to inclusion. Define the monoidal product as union:
X\otimes Y=X\cup Y
. The subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
consisting of open subsets is monoidal. It has a monoidal subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}
of the intervals
(0,x)
, where
x\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geqslant 0}
. By identifying
(0,x)
with
x
we can interpret
{\mathbf{P}}
as the standard normal order on
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geqslant 0}
with the monoidal product
x\otimes y=\max(x,y)
. The monoidal category
({\mathbf{P}},\otimes)
is coclosed with the cohom-functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(x,y)=\begin{cases}0,&x\geqslant y;\\
y,&x<y.\end{cases}

(2.80)

Nevertheless, the monoidal category
({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},\otimes)
is not coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
. Theorem
2.26
is not applicable here, since the subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
is not coreflective. The whole category
{\mathbf{C}}
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
but with another cohom-functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(x,y)=\{a>0\mid x\leqslant a<y\}=\begin{cases}\emptyset,&x\geqslant y;\\
(0,y),&x=0;\\
[x,y),&0<x<y.\end{cases}

(2.81)

This gives two counterexamples: for
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
. The former one shows that Prop.
2.20
does not work without the conditions on the cohom-functor, while the latter one tells us that it is not necessary condition and the internal (co)hom-functor for a monoidal subcategory may differ from the internal (co)hom-functor for the whole category.

3
General representation theory

Here we generalise the approach described in
[
S21
, § 3]
for the case of relatively (co)closed monoidal categories.

3.1
Representations in relatively closed monoidal categories

By following the ideas of
[
S21
, § 3.2]
we first consider the categories of (co)representations.

3.1.1.

Category of representations.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
be monoid in a relatively closed monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes,I)
with a parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}
, where
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
representation
of the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
on an object
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
is a morphism
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
in the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. This is a morphism
\rho\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
in
{\mathbf{C}}
such that the diagrams

\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\rho\otimes\rho}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895ptc_{V}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)}

\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{u_{V}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)}

are commutative. Let
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
and
\tau\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)
be representations of the same monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
on the objects
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
W\in{\mathbf{P}}
respectively. A morphism between these representations is a morphism
V\to W
in
{\mathbf{C}}
making the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V},f)}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)}

Denote by
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
the category of representations of the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
with their morphisms. The objects of this category are the pairs
(V,\rho)
, where
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
is a representation of
{\mathbb{M}}
on
V
. A morphism
(V,\rho)\to(W,\tau)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
is a morphism
f\colon V\to W
in
{\mathbf{P}}
respecting the representations in the sense of diagram (
3.1
).

Proposition 3.2
.

The forgetful functor

\displaystyle G\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\to{\mathbf{P}},

\displaystyle(V,\rho)\mapsto V,

\displaystyle f\mapsto f,

(3.13)

reflects isomorphisms: if
(V,\rho)
,
(W,\rho)
are objects of the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
and a morphism
f\colon(V,\rho)\to(W,\tau)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
is given by an isomorphism
f\colon V\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}W
in
{\mathbf{P}}
, then
f\colon(V,\rho)\to(W,\tau)
is an isomorphism in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
.

Proof.
Let
f^{-1}\colon W\to V
be the inverse morphism for
f
. Then, by composing the diagram (
3.1
) with
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f^{-1},f^{-1})\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
we see that
f^{-1}
gives a morphism
(W,\tau)\to(V,\rho)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
. ∎

Remark 3.3
.

Consider the composition functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\xrightarrow{G^{\mathrm{op}}\times G}{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits}{\mathbf{C}},

(3.14)

where
G
is the forgetful functor (
3.13
). The commutative diagram (
3.1
) means exactly that the formula
\alpha_{(V,\rho)}=\rho\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)
defines a dinatural transformation
\alpha
from the constant functor
X
to (
3.14
).

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes,I)
be a relatively coclosed monoidal category with the parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}
. Consider a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
, where
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
.

Definition 3.4
.

A
corepresentation
of the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}
on an object
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
is a morphism
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\to{\mathbb{O}}
in the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. This is a morphism
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\to X
in
{\mathbf{C}}
such that the diagrams

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\omega}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt\omega\otimes\omega}
\textstyle{X\otimes X}

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\omega}
\scriptstyle{v_{V}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}}
\textstyle{I}

are commutative. Denote by
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})
the category whose objects are the pairs
(V,\omega)
, where
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
\omega
is a corepresentation of
{\mathbb{O}}
on
V
, and morphisms
(V,\omega)\to(W,\nu)
are morphisms
f\colon V\to W
in
{\mathbf{P}}
such that

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W},f)}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\nu}
\textstyle{X}

The categorical duality allows us to pass from relatively closed categories to relatively coclosed categories (see p.
2.4
). For any monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
we have the correspondence

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}},

(3.19)

which identifies the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
in
{\mathbf{C}}
with the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
in the opposite monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}=({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
, where
\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X\otimes X
and
\varepsilon_{X}\colon X\to I_{\mathbf{C}}
are the morphisms
\mu_{X}\colon X\otimes X\to X
and
\eta_{X}\colon I_{\mathbf{C}}\to X
regarded as morphisms in
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
If
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is symmetric and closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
, then the categories of representations of the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
and corepresentations of the corresponding comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}
are opposite to each other. A representation
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
on
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
is identified with the corepresentation
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\to{\mathbb{O}}
by the functor (
3.19
) as morphisms of monoids and comonoids respectively, where
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)
are end-monoid and coend-monoid for
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
respectively identified by (
3.19
). Moreover, if
\tau\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)
is a representation of
{\mathbb{M}}
on
W\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
\nu\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(W)\to{\mathbb{O}}
is the corresponding corepresentation, then a morphism
f\colon V\to W
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is a morphism
(V,\rho)\to(W,\tau)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
iff it is a morphism
(W,\nu)\to(V,\omega)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})
. Thus we have the isomorphism of categories

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})=\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}}({\mathbb{O}})^{\mathrm{op}},

(3.20)

which identifies
(V,\rho)
with
(V,\omega)
and
f\colon(V,\rho)\to(W,\tau)
with
f\colon(W,\nu)\to(V,\omega)
.

3.1.2.

Representations as actions.

Recall that for any (symmetric) monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
the pairs
(V,a)
of an object
V\in{\mathbf{C}}
with an action
a\colon X\otimes V\to V
of a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
form a category denoted by
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
. For a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
the pairs
(V,\delta)
of
V\in{\mathbf{C}}
with a coaction
\delta\colon V\to X\otimes V
form the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}})
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Fix a parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
. If
{\mathbf{C}}
is relatively closed, then for any
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
the bijection

\displaystyle\theta_{X,V,V}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X\otimes V,V\big)

(3.21)

establishes one-to-one correspondence between representations
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
and actions
a\colon X\otimes V\to V
of
{\mathbb{M}}
on
V
, i.e.
\rho\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)
is a representation iff
a=\theta(\rho)
is an action. A morphism
f\colon V\to W
is a morphism of representations
(V,\rho)\to(W,\tau)
iff it is a morphism of actions
(V,a)\to(W,b)
, where
b=\theta(\tau)
. Thus we can consider
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
as a full subcategory of
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
via the fully faithful functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\hookrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}}),

\displaystyle(V,\rho)\mapsto\big(V,\theta(\rho)\big).

(3.22)

Analogously, if
{\mathbf{C}}
is relatively coclosed, then the bijections

\displaystyle\vartheta_{V,V,X}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V),X\big)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(V,X\otimes V\big),

\displaystyle V\in{\mathbf{P}},

(3.23)

give the fully faithful functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})\hookrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}}),

\displaystyle(V,\omega)\mapsto\big(V,\vartheta(\omega)\big).

(3.24)

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is closed or coclosed (i.e.
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{C}}
), then the functor (
3.22
) or, respectively, (
3.24
) is an equivalence (more exactly, isomorphism) of categories.

Proof.
Repeats the proof of
[
S21
, Lemmas 3.3, 3.5]
. ∎

In the relatively closed case the identity morphism

\displaystyle X=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)

(3.25)

is a representation of the monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)=(X,c_{V},u_{V})
on
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
.
The corresponding action is
\theta(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,V}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes V\to V
, which was mentioned in p.
2.4
.
In the relatively coclosed case we have an analogical corepresentation

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)=X

(3.26)

of the comonoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)=(X,d_{V},v_{V})
on the object
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
. It corresponds to the coaction
\vartheta(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,V}\colon V\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\otimes V
.

3.2
Tensor product of representations

The monoidal product of representations (actions) of a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
is introduced by means of an additional structure on
{\mathbb{M}}
, namely by a structure of comonoid which turn
{\mathbb{M}}
into a bimonoid. In
[
S21
]
we considered monoidal product in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
via a monoidal product in
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
. In the present work we define and study the former monoidal product without the notion of (co)action, but then we show that these two ways give the same result. Note also that here we consider more general case then in
[
S21
]
, the case of relatively (co)closed monoidal categories.

In this subsection we always suppose that the monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes,I)
is symmetric. The corresponding symmetric structure is a natural isomorphism, which we denote by
\sigma
. It has components
\sigma=\sigma_{X,Y}\colon X\otimes Y\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}Y\otimes X
. Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be objects of
{\mathbf{C}}
. We use the following notation: for
a=1,\ldots,n-1
we denote by
\sigma^{(a,a+1)}
the isomorphism

X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{a}\otimes X_{a+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n}\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{a+1}\otimes X_{a}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n}
, where
\sigma

acts on the
a
-th and
(a+1)
-st factors, that is

\sigma^{(a,a+1)}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{\otimes(a-1)}\otimes\sigma\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{\otimes(n-a-1)}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X_{a-1}}\otimes\sigma_{X_{a},X_{a+1}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X_{a+2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X_{n}}.

3.2.1.

Monoidal structure on the category of monoids.

Recall that the symmetric structure of
{\mathbf{C}}
allows to define a monoidal structure on the category of monoids
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. Let us describe this structure in details.

The monoidal product of two monoids
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X}),(Y,\mu_{Y},\eta_{Y})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
is the triple
(X\otimes Y,\mu_{X\otimes Y},\eta_{X\otimes Y})
, where
\mu_{X\otimes Y}
and
\eta_{X\otimes Y}
are defined by the commutative diagrams

\textstyle{X\otimes Y\otimes X\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X\otimes Y}}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\otimes Y\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{Y}}
\textstyle{X\otimes Y}

\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X\otimes Y}}
\textstyle{I\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y}}
\textstyle{X\otimes Y}

One can check that it is also a monoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
. The monoidal product of morphisms in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
is obtained as follows.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X}),(Y,\mu_{Y},\eta_{Y}),(X^{\prime},\mu_{X^{\prime}},\eta_{X^{\prime}}),(Y^{\prime},\mu_{Y^{\prime}},\eta_{Y^{\prime}})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. If
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
are morphisms of monoids
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\to(X^{\prime},\mu_{X^{\prime}},\eta_{X^{\prime}})
and
(Y,\mu_{Y},\eta_{Y})\to(Y^{\prime},\mu_{Y^{\prime}},\eta_{Y^{\prime}})
, then
f\otimes g\colon X\otimes Y\to X^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime}
is a morphism

(X\otimes Y,\mu_{X\otimes Y},\eta_{X\otimes Y})\to(X^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime},\mu_{X^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime}},\eta_{X^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime}}).

Proof.
It follows from the commutative diagrams

\textstyle{X\otimes Y\otimes X\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\scriptstyle{f\otimes g\otimes f\otimes g}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\otimes Y\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{f\otimes f\otimes g\otimes g}
\textstyle{X\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\otimes g}
\textstyle{I\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\eta_{Y^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime}\otimes X^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\otimes X^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\mu_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mu_{Y^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\otimes Y^{\prime}}

and the definitions (
3.2
). ∎

Thus we have a bifunctor
-\otimes-\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\times\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. It is easy to check that
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})=\big(\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}}),\otimes\big)
is a symmetric monoidal category with the unit object
I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})}=(I,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I})
. The category of comonoids
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
is equipped with a structure of symmetric monoidal category in the dual way.

3.2.2.

Natural transformation
\pi
.

Until the end of this subsection we suppose that the subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
is monoidal (this condition is reduced to
I\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
V\otimes V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}\;\forall\,V,V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
, since
{\mathbf{P}}
is a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{C}}
). Then for any
V,V^{\prime},W,W^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
we have the hom-object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime})
. In particular, we have the monoids
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I)
. By Prop.
2.25
the latter monoid is the unit object:
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I)=(I,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I})
. We are going to construct some useful morphisms of monoids
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})
.

Let us use the notation
[V,W]:=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
for briefness.
Consider the composition

\displaystyle[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{(23)}}[V,W]\otimes V\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits}W\otimes W^{\prime},

(3.35)

where
V,V^{\prime},W,W^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
. By applying the bijection

\theta^{-1}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big([V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\\
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big([V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}],[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]\big)

to (
3.35
) we get the morphism
\pi=\pi^{V,V^{\prime}}_{W,W^{\prime}}=\theta^{-1}\big((\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\big)
of the form

\displaystyle\pi\colon[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\to[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}].

(3.36)

This is a unique morphism making the diagram

\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\scriptstyle{\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes V\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{W\otimes W^{\prime}}

commute.

Proposition 3.7
.

The morphisms
\pi^{V,V^{\prime}}_{W,W^{\prime}}
are natural in all four arguments
V,V^{\prime},W,W^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
.

Proof.
We need to prove the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f\otimes f^{\prime},g\otimes g^{\prime})}
\textstyle{[U,Z]\otimes[U^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{[U\otimes U^{\prime},Z\otimes Z^{\prime}]}

for arbitrary morphisms
f\colon U\to V
,
f\colon U^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
,
g\colon W\to Z
and
g^{\prime}\colon W^{\prime}\to Z^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{P}}
. Let us apply
\theta
to the compositions in this diagram. This amounts to monoidal multiplication of each object and arrow in the diagram by
U\otimes U^{\prime}
from the right and composition with
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\colon[U\otimes U^{\prime},Z\otimes Z^{\prime}]\otimes U\otimes U^{\prime}\to Z\otimes Z^{\prime}
. By using the commutativity of (
3.2
) and naturality of
\sigma
we get the equivalent diagram

\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes U\otimes U^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]\otimes U\otimes U^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f\otimes f^{\prime},g\otimes g^{\prime})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes U\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes U^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{[U\otimes U^{\prime},Z\otimes Z^{\prime}]\otimes U\otimes U^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{[U,Z]\otimes U\otimes[U^{\prime},Z^{\prime}]\otimes U^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{Z\otimes Z^{\prime}}

By virtue of Prop.
2.24
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f\otimes f^{\prime},g\otimes g^{\prime})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime}}\big)=\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f\otimes f^{\prime},g\otimes g^{\prime})\big)=(g\otimes g^{\prime})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f\otimes f^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime}}\big)=\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)\big)\otimes\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\big)=

\displaystyle\big(g\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f)\big)\otimes\big(g^{\prime}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f^{\prime})\big),

so the diagram (
3.2
) is equivalent to

\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes U\otimes U^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f\otimes f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes U\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes U^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes V\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{W\otimes W^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\otimes g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z\otimes Z^{\prime}}

By drawing the arrow
\sigma^{(23)}
from the top right corner to the down left one we see that the commutativity of the diagram (
3.2
) follows from the naturality of
\sigma
and commutativity of (
3.2
). ∎

3.2.3.

Properties of
\pi
.

The natural transformation
\pi
is agreed with the internal composition (
2.37
) in the following sense.

Proposition 3.8
.

For any objects
U,V,W,U^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
we get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[U,V]\otimes[U^{\prime},V^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\scriptstyle{\pi\otimes\pi}
\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[U,V]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[U^{\prime},V^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{U,V,W}\otimes c_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]\otimes[U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{[U,W]\otimes[U^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{[U\otimes U^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]}

where
c_{U,V,W}
are the internal compositions (
2.37
).

Proof.
We need to show the equality of two morphisms
X\to[U\otimes U^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]
, where
X=[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[U,V]\otimes[U^{\prime},V^{\prime}]
. One can show that their images under
\theta_{X,U\otimes U^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}
coincide with each other by using the naturality of
\sigma
and the diagrams (
3.2
), (
2.4
) in an appropriate way. However, the proof in terms of diagrams would take too much space. We present it more compactly. By using the formula (
2.35
) and the definition of
c_{U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}
we obtain

\theta\big(c_{U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}\cdot(\pi\otimes\pi)\big)=\theta(c_{U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}})\cdot(\pi\otimes\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime}})=\\
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime}})\cdot(\pi\otimes\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime}}).

(3.51)

Note that
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime}})\cdot(\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime}}=(\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime}})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime}})
, so (
3.51
) equals

\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}}\cdot(\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime}})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime}})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime}})=\\
(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U,V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(45)},

(3.52)

where we used the commutative diagram (
3.2
). Then, by taking into account the equality
\sigma^{(23)}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U,V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime}})=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U,V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(34)}\cdot\sigma^{(23)}
and again the definition of internal composition we can rewrite (
3.52
) in the form

(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime},W^{\prime}})\cdot(c_{U,V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U}\otimes c_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(34)}\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\cdot\sigma^{(45)}=\\
(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime},W^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\cdot(c_{U,V,W}\otimes c_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}=\\
\theta(\pi)\cdot(c_{U,V,W}\otimes c_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime}})\cdot(\sigma^{(23)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U\otimes U^{\prime}})=\theta\big(\pi\cdot(c_{U,V,W}\otimes c_{U^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\big).

Since
\theta
is a bijection, the proposition is proved. ∎

Let us establish ‘associativity’ of the natural transformation
\pi
.

Proposition 3.9
.

For any
V,W,V^{\prime},W^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[V^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]\otimes[V^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime}\otimes V^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime}\otimes W^{\prime\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime}\otimes V^{\prime\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}\otimes W^{\prime\prime}]}

Proof.
By using the diagram (
3.2
) one can show that the morphism
\theta\big(\pi\cdot(\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\big)
coincides with the composition
[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[V^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime}]\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{(23)}\sigma^{(45)}\sigma^{(34)}}[V,W]\otimes V\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V^{\prime}\otimes[V^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime}]\otimes V^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime}}}W\otimes W^{\prime}\otimes W^{\prime\prime}
. Analogously, one derives that

\theta\big(\pi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi)\big)
coincides with the same composition. ∎

Proposition 3.10
.

The natural transformation
\pi
is in agreement with the symmetric structure
\sigma
in the sense that the diagram

\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\sigma_{V^{\prime},V},\sigma_{W,W^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[V,W]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{[V^{\prime}\otimes V,W^{\prime}\otimes W]}

commutes, where

\displaystyle\sigma=\sigma_{[V,W],[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]}\colon[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\to[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[V,W],

(3.61)

\displaystyle\sigma_{V^{\prime},V}\colon V^{\prime}\otimes V\to V\otimes V^{\prime},\quad\quad\sigma_{W,W^{\prime}}\colon W\otimes W^{\prime}\to W^{\prime}\otimes W

(3.62)

are the corresponding components of the natural transformation
\sigma
.

Proof.
Consider the morphism
\sigma^{-1}=\sigma_{[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}],[V,W]}
inverse to (
3.61
). Together with the morphisms (
3.62
) and naturality of
\theta^{-1}
it gives the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big([V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{(\sigma^{-1}\otimes\sigma_{V^{\prime},V})^{*}\cdot(\sigma_{W,W^{\prime}})_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big([V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}],[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\sigma^{-1})^{*}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\sigma_{V^{\prime},V},\sigma_{W,W^{\prime}})_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big([V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[V,W]\otimes V^{\prime}\otimes V,W^{\prime}\otimes W\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{-1}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big([V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[V,W],[V^{\prime}\otimes V,W^{\prime}\otimes W]\big)}

Recall that
\pi=\theta\big((\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\big)
. The commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\otimes\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes V\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{[V,W]\otimes V,[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{W\otimes W^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{W,W^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes[V,W]\otimes V^{\prime}\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\textstyle{[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\otimes V^{\prime}\otimes[V,W]\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{W^{\prime}\otimes W}

implies that the left vertical arrow of the diagram (
3.2
) maps the morphism
(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}
to
(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}
. Hence, by applying (
3.2
) to this morphism we obtain the needed equality
\pi=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\sigma_{V^{\prime},V},\sigma_{W,W^{\prime}})\cdot\pi\cdot\sigma^{-1}
. ∎

3.2.4.

The morphisms
\pi
for internal ends.

If
W=V
and
W^{\prime}=V^{\prime}
, then the morphism (
3.36
) takes the form

\displaystyle\pi\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime}).

(3.71)

The domain object of this morphism is equipped with a structure of monoid as a monoidal product of two monoids; namely, the multiplication morphism for this monoid is
(c_{V}\otimes c_{V^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}
, while the unit is
u_{V}\otimes u_{V^{\prime}}
. The codomain of (
3.71
) is a monoid with the structure
(c_{V\otimes V^{\prime}},u_{V\otimes V^{\prime}})
. Prop.
3.8
for
U=W=V
and
U^{\prime}=W^{\prime}=V^{\prime}
means exactly that (
3.71
) preserves the multiplications. Let us prove that it also preserves the units.

Lemma 3.11
.

The following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{I\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{V}\otimes u_{V^{\prime}}\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{V\otimes V^{\prime}}\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})}

Proof.
Due to the formula (
2.34
) and commutativity of the diagram (
3.2
) we obtain

\theta\big(\pi\cdot(u_{V}\otimes u_{V^{\prime}})\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime},V\otimes V^{\prime}}\cdot(\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime}})\cdot(u_{V}\otimes u_{V^{\prime}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime}})=\\
(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\cdot(u_{V}\otimes u_{V^{\prime}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}})=(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot(u_{V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\otimes u_{V^{\prime}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}.

(3.76)

From the diagram (
2.4
) we see that the right hand side of the formula (
3.76
) is the identification
I\otimes I\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}=V\otimes V^{\prime}
, so it equals
\theta(u_{V\otimes V^{\prime}})
. ∎

Proposition 3.12
.

The morphisms (
3.71
) are morphisms of monoids.

Proof.
This is a direct consequence of Prop.
3.8
and Lemma
3.11
. ∎

Proposition 3.13
.

Both morphisms

\displaystyle\pi^{I,V}_{I,W}\colon[I,I]\otimes[V,W]\to[I\otimes V,I\otimes W],

\displaystyle\pi^{V,I}_{W,I}\colon[V,W]\otimes[I,I]\to[V\otimes I,W\otimes I]

are equal to
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{[V,W]}
. In particular, the morphism (
3.71
) for
V=I
or
V^{\prime}=I
takes the form of the identification

\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})=I\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})
or, respectively, it coincides with

\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I)=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes I=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
.

Proof.
By substituting
V^{\prime}=W^{\prime}=I
(or
V=W=I
) to the diagram (
3.2
) and using the identifications
I\otimes V=V=I\otimes V
,
[I,I]=I
we see that
\pi=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{[V,W]}
makes it commute. Then the statement of the proposition follows from the uniqueness of
\pi
. ∎

Lemma 3.14
.

The morphisms (
3.71
) make the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\sigma_{V^{\prime},V},\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime}\otimes V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},V^{\prime}\otimes V]}

Proof.
This follows from Prop.
3.10
and the equalities
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\sigma_{V^{\prime},V},\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}})=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma_{V^{\prime},V},\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}})=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}})
. ∎

3.2.5.

Natural transformation
\varkappa
.

In the relatively coclosed case we can consider cohom-objects
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime})
for any objects
V,V^{\prime},W,W^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
. In particular, the have comonoids
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(I)=I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})}=(I,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I})
.

To define morphisms dual to (
3.36
) we apply the bijection

\vartheta^{-1}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(W\otimes W^{\prime},\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\\
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}),\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\big)

to the composition

W\otimes W^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes V\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\otimes V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{(23)}}\\
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}.

This gives the morphism

\varkappa=\varkappa^{V,V^{\prime}}_{W,W^{\prime}}=\theta^{-1}\big(\sigma^{(23)}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits)\big)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime})\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},W^{\prime}).

By applying duality principle to Prop.
3.7
we conclude that these morphisms are also natural in
V,V^{\prime},W,W^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
.

The natural transformation
\varkappa
is coassociative in the sense similar to Prop.
3.9
, but with the opposite arrows:
(\varkappa\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\varkappa=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\varkappa)\cdot\varkappa
.

In the case
W=V
and
W^{\prime}=V^{\prime}
it reads

\displaystyle\varkappa\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V^{\prime}).

(3.81)

Proposition 3.15
.

The morphisms (
3.81
) are morphisms of comonoids.

Proof.
It follows from Prop.
3.12
by the duality principle. ∎

3.2.6.

Tensor product of representations of bimonoids.

A bimonoid in the symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}
is
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
, where
\mu_{X}
,
\eta_{X}
,
\Delta_{X}
and
\varepsilon_{X}
are multiplication, unit, comultiplication and counit respectively on the object
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
. By considering
{\mathbb{B}}
as a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
we obtain the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})=\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
. If
{\mathbf{C}}
is closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
, then we have the subcategory
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})=\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\subset\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
. Here we describe how the comonoid structure
(\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
gives a monoidal structure on the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
.

Let
\rho\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
and
\rho^{\prime}\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})
be two representations of the bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}
(i.e. of the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
) on the objects
V,V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Define a morphism
\rho^{\prime\prime}\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})
as the composition

\displaystyle X\xrightarrow{\Delta_{X}}X\otimes X\xrightarrow{\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime}).

(3.82)

Lemma 3.16
.

The morphism (
3.82
) is a representation of
{\mathbb{B}}
on
V\otimes V^{\prime}
.

Proof.
Since
{\mathbb{B}}
is a bimonoid,
\Delta_{X}
is a morphism of monoids
{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}\otimes{\mathbb{M}}
. From Prop.
3.6
we obtain the monoid morphism
{\mathbb{M}}\otimes{\mathbb{M}}\xrightarrow{\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})
. Due to Prop.
3.12
the formula (
3.82
) gives a morphism
{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. ∎

We call the representation (
3.82
) the
tensor product
of the representations
\rho
,
\rho^{\prime}
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\rho
,
\rho^{\prime}
,
\tau
,
\tau^{\prime}
be representations of
{\mathbb{B}}
on
V,V^{\prime},W,W^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Denote the tensor products of
\rho
,
\rho^{\prime}
and of
\tau
,
\tau^{\prime}
by
\rho^{\prime\prime}
and by
\tau^{\prime\prime}
respectively. If morphisms
f\colon V\to W
and
f^{\prime}\colon V^{\prime}\to W^{\prime}
are morphisms of representations, i.e. morphisms
f\colon(V,\rho)\to(W,\tau)
and
f^{\prime}\colon(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\to(W^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
, then
f\otimes f^{\prime}\colon V\otimes V^{\prime}\to W\otimes W^{\prime}
is a morphism
f\otimes f^{\prime}\colon(V\otimes V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime\prime})\to(W\otimes W^{\prime},\tau^{\prime\prime})
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
.

Proof.
By definition
3.1
the fact that
f
and
f^{\prime}
are morphisms of representations means the equalities
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,f)\cdot\rho=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\tau
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,f^{\prime})\cdot\rho^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f^{\prime},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\tau^{\prime}
. By taking into account these equalities and the naturality of
\pi
(Prop.
3.7
) we obtain the commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\tau\otimes\tau^{\prime}}
\textstyle{[V,V]\otimes[V^{\prime},V^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,f)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,f\otimes f^{\prime})}
\textstyle{[W,W]\otimes[W^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f^{\prime},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)}
\textstyle{[V,W]\otimes[V^{\prime},W^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W\otimes W^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f\otimes f^{\prime},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},W\otimes W^{\prime}]}

Composition with
\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X\otimes X
gives
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,f\otimes f^{\prime})\cdot\rho^{\prime\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f\otimes f^{\prime},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\tau^{\prime\prime}
. ∎

Theorem 3.18
.

For any bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}
in a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
which is closed relative to a monoidal subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
is equipped with the monoidal structure in the following way. The monoidal product of objects
(V,\rho)
and
(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
is the object
(V^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime\prime})
, where
V^{\prime\prime}=V\otimes V^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\prime\prime}\colon{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime\prime})
is defined by (
3.82
). The monoidal product of morphisms
f
and
g
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
coincides with their monoidal product in
{\mathbf{P}}
. The unit object of
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
is
(I_{\mathbf{C}},\varepsilon_{X})
. If
{\mathbb{B}}
is cocommutative (i.e.
\sigma\cdot\Delta_{X}=\Delta_{X}
), then the monoidal category
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
is symmetric and
\sigma_{(V,\rho),(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}=\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}}
.

Proof.
The monoidal operation on objects and on morphisms of
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
is defined correctly due to Lemmas
3.16
and
3.17
respectively. Let us check its associativity. Consider representations
\rho,\rho^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}
of
{\mathbb{B}}
on
V,V^{\prime},\widetilde{V}\in{\mathbf{P}}
respectively and let
\rho^{\prime\prime}
be the tensor product of
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
. The sequential monoidal products of objects
(V,\rho)
,
(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
and
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\rho})
is given by the tensor product of the representations
\rho^{\prime\prime}
and
\widetilde{\rho}
, this is a representation of
{\mathbb{B}}
on
V\otimes V^{\prime}\otimes\widetilde{V}
of the form

\pi\cdot(\rho^{\prime\prime}\otimes\widetilde{\rho})\cdot\Delta_{X}=\pi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\widetilde{\rho})\cdot(\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\Delta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\Delta_{X}=\\
\pi\cdot(\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}\otimes\widetilde{\rho})\cdot(\Delta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\Delta_{X}.

(3.83)

By taking the tensor products in another order we obtain

\pi\cdot(\rho\otimes\widehat{\rho})\cdot\Delta_{X}=\pi\cdot(\rho\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\rho^{\prime}\otimes\widetilde{\rho})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\Delta_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}=\\
\pi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi)\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}\otimes\widetilde{\rho})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\Delta_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X},

(3.84)

where
\widehat{\rho}
is the tensor product of
\rho^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\rho}
. The representation (
3.83
) coincides with (
3.84
) by virtue of coassociativity of
\Delta_{X}
and Prop.
3.9
. The associativity of the monoidal operation on morphisms follows from the associativity of the monoidal product in
{\mathbf{P}}
.

By definition of bimonoid the counit
\varepsilon_{X}
is in agreement with the monoid structure
(\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
, i.e. the diagrams

\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\varepsilon_{X}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}}
\textstyle{I\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{I}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I}}
\textstyle{I}

\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{u_{I}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}}
\textstyle{I}

commute. This means exactly that
\varepsilon_{X}\colon X\to I=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I)
is a representation of
{\mathbb{B}}
on the object
I
. Let us check that
(I,\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
is a unit object. The tensor product of a representation
\rho\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
with the representation
\varepsilon_{X}\colon X\to I=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I)
equals
\pi^{I,V}_{I,V}\cdot(\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\rho)\cdot\Delta_{X}=\pi^{I,V}_{I,V}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{I}\otimes\rho)\cdot(\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}
. Due to
(\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}
and Prop.
3.13
it coincides with
\rho
, so that
(I,\varepsilon_{X})\otimes(V,\rho)=(V,\rho)
. The identification
(V,\rho)\otimes(I,\varepsilon_{X})=(V,\rho)
is checked in the same way.

If
{\mathbb{B}}
is cocommutative, then due to the naturality of
\sigma
and Lemma
3.14
the diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\pi}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}\otimes\rho\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\pi}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime}\otimes V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{[V\otimes V^{\prime},V^{\prime}\otimes V]}

commutes, so
\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}}\colon V\otimes V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}V^{\prime}\otimes V
is a morphism
(V,\rho)\otimes(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\to(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\otimes(V,\rho)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
. This is an isomorphism in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
, since its inverse
\sigma_{V^{\prime},V}
is a morphism
(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\otimes(V,\rho)\to(V,\rho)\otimes(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
. ∎

Let us draw the dual picture. The concept of bimonoid is self-dual. By considering
{\mathbb{B}}
as a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
we obtain the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})=\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}})
. If
{\mathbf{C}}
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
, then we define
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})=\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})\subset\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
. This subcategory is a monoidal category whose monoidal structure is given by the morphisms
\mu_{X},\eta_{X}
. To describe this structure we dualise Lemma
3.16
and Theorem
3.18
.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\to X
and
\omega^{\prime}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\to X
be corepresentations of the bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}
(as a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}
) on the objects
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
respectively. Then the morphism
\omega^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})\to X
defined as the composition

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V\otimes V^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\varkappa}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\omega\otimes\omega^{\prime}}X\otimes X\xrightarrow{\mu_{X}}X

(3.85)

is a corepresentation of
{\mathbb{B}}
on
V\otimes V^{\prime}
.

The corepresentation (
3.85
) is called the
tensor product
of the corepresentations
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
.

Theorem 3.20
.

For any bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}
in a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
which is coclosed relative to a monoidal subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
is equipped with the monoidal structure. The monoidal product on objects has the form
(V,\omega)\otimes(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})=(V^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime})
, where
V^{\prime\prime}=V\otimes V^{\prime}
and
\omega^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V^{\prime\prime})\to{\mathbb{B}}
is the corepresentation (
3.85
). On morphisms it is given by the monoidal product
\otimes
of
{\mathbf{C}}
. The unit object of
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
is
(I_{\mathbf{C}},\eta_{X})
. If
{\mathbb{B}}
is commutative (i.e.
\mu_{X}\cdot\sigma=\mu_{X}
), then the monoidal category
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
is symmetric and
\sigma_{(V,\omega),(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})}=\sigma_{V,V^{\prime}}
.

3.2.7.

Tensor product of actions.

Since Theorem
3.5
identifies the representations with actions, Lemma
3.16
allows to define tensor product of actions of the bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}
on objects of
{\mathbf{P}}
in the relatively closed case. This operation can be written explicitly via the coproduct
\Delta_{X}
. In the relatively coclosed case we have tensor product of coactions of the bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}
on objects of
{\mathbf{P}}
, which can be written via
\mu_{X}
.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
\rho\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
and
\rho^{\prime}\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})
be representations of
{\mathbb{B}}
on
V,V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
\rho^{\prime\prime}\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime\prime})
be their tensor product (
3.82
), where
V^{\prime\prime}=V\otimes V^{\prime}
. Consider the corresponding actions
a=\theta(\rho)\colon X\otimes V\to V
,
a^{\prime}=\theta(\rho^{\prime})\colon X\otimes V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime\prime}=\theta(\rho^{\prime\prime})\colon X\otimes V^{\prime\prime}\to V^{\prime\prime}
. Then
a^{\prime\prime}
coincides with the composition

\displaystyle X\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\Delta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}X\otimes X\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{(23)}}X\otimes V\otimes X\otimes V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a\otimes a^{\prime}}V\otimes V^{\prime}.

(3.86)

Dually, let
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\to X
and
\omega^{\prime}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})\to X
be corepresentations of the bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}
on the objects
V,V^{\prime}
. Let
\delta=\vartheta(\omega)\colon V\to X\otimes V
and
\delta^{\prime}=\vartheta(\omega^{\prime})\colon V^{\prime}\to X\otimes V^{\prime}
. Then the coaction
\delta^{\prime\prime}=\vartheta(\omega^{\prime\prime})\colon V^{\prime\prime}\to X\otimes V^{\prime\prime}
corresponding to the tensor product (
3.85
) coincides with

\displaystyle V\otimes V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\delta\otimes\delta^{\prime}}X\otimes V\otimes X\otimes V\xrightarrow{\sigma^{(23)}}X\otimes X\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mu_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}X\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}.

(3.87)

Proof.
By sequentially applying (
2.35
), definition of
\pi
, naturality of
\sigma
and the formula (
2.34
) we derive

\theta\big(\pi\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})\big)=\theta(\pi)\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime}})=(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}})=\\
(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot(\rho\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\otimes\rho^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}=(\theta(\rho)\otimes\theta(\rho^{\prime}))\cdot\sigma^{(23)}=(a\otimes a^{\prime})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}.

(3.88)

By using (
2.35
) again we obtain

\displaystyle a^{\prime\prime}=\theta(\rho^{\prime\prime})=\theta\big(\pi\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})\cdot\Delta_{X}\big)=\theta\big(\pi\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})\big)\cdot(\Delta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime}}),

(3.89)

Substitution of (
3.88
) to (
3.89
) yields (
3.86
). ∎

Remark 3.22
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{P}}
is a closed monoidal category, then the equivalence (
3.22
) induces a monoidal structure from the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbb{B}})
to
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
. It is claimed in
[
Par
]
(without proof) that for arbitrary symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}
and a bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
is monoidal with respect to the monoidal product defined by the formula (
3.86
). Dually,
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
is a monoidal category with the structure defined by (
3.87
). By this logic, Prop.
3.21
means that the embeddings (
3.22
) and (
3.24
) are strict monoidal functors.

3.3
Translation of representations by a functor

If a functor between two relatively (co)closed monoidal categories respects their structures in an appropriate way, then this functor induces functors between the categories of (co)monoids, bimonoids, (co)actions and (co)representations. We describe the functor which translates (co)representations in details by following ideas of
[
S21
, § 3.3]
(we adapt them to the case of relatively (co)closed monoidal categories). As above we do not use the actions and their connection with representations.
For the case of bimonoid we study the translation of the tensor product of (co)representations and get a monoidal structure of the corresponding induced functor.

3.3.1.

Translation of internal hom.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be symmetric monoidal categories. Let
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
be their full subcategories. Suppose that
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
are relatively closed with the parametrising categories
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}
respectively.

Let
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be a lax monoidal functor: it consists of a functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
, a morphism
\varphi\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FI_{\mathbf{C}}
and a natural transformation
\phi
with the components
\phi_{X,Y}\colon FX\odot FY\to F(X\otimes Y)
satisfying necessary conditions.

Suppose
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
. Then we can define the hom-object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FW)
in
{\mathbf{D}}
for any
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
and relate it with the image
F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)
by a natural morphism as follows (see
[
Bor2
, Prop. 6.4.5]
for the closed case).

Let us apply the right adjunction bijection

\displaystyle\theta^{-1}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)\odot FV,FW\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big),\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FW)\big)

to the composition

\displaystyle F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)\odot FV\xrightarrow{\phi}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes V\big)\xrightarrow{F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W})}FW

(3.90)

and denote the result by
\Phi_{V,W}=\theta^{-1}\big(F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W})\cdot\phi_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W),V}\big)
. This is a unique morphism

\displaystyle\Phi_{V,W}\colon F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FW)

(3.91)

such that the diagram

\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)\odot FV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V,W}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FV}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FW)\odot FV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{FV,FW}}
\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes FV\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{VW})}
\textstyle{FW}

commutes.

Proposition 3.23
.

For any objects
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
and morphism
f\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
in
{\mathbf{C}}
we have the formula

\displaystyle\theta\big(\Phi_{V,W}\cdot F(f)\big)=F\big(\theta(f)\big)\cdot\phi_{X,V}.

(3.96)

Proof.
The formula (
3.96
) follows from the commutativity of the diagram (
3.3
) and the naturality of
\phi_{X,Y}
in the first argument (see the proof of
[
S21
, Prop. 3.9]
). ∎

In the closed case the naturality of
\Phi
is due to the point (1) of
[
Bor2
, Prop. 6.4.5]
, which was claimed without a (detailed) proof.
For the relative case we deduce the naturality of
\Phi
by using Prop.
3.23
and the naturality of
\phi_{X,Y}
in
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
.

Proposition 3.24
.

The morphisms (
3.91
) are natural in
V,W\in{\mathbf{P}}
, i.e. for any morphisms
f\colon V^{\prime}\to V
and
g\colon W\to W^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{P}}
we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V,W}}
\scriptstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)\big)}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FW)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Ff,Fg)}
\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV^{\prime},FW^{\prime})}

Proof.
By using the formulae (
3.96
), (
2.67
) and (
2.35
) we obtain

\displaystyle\theta\Big(\Phi_{V^{\prime},W^{\prime}}\cdot F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)\big)\Big)=F\Big(\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)\big)\Big)\cdot\phi_{X,V^{\prime}}=Fg\cdot F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot F(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f)\cdot\phi_{X,V},

\displaystyle\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Ff,Fg)\cdot\Phi_{V,W}\big)=\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Ff,Fg)\big)\cdot(\Phi_{V,W}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=Fg\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\Phi_{V,W}\odot Ff),

where
X=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
. The right hand sides of these equalities coincide with each other due to the naturality of
\phi
and the commutativity of (
3.3
):

\displaystyle F(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f)\cdot\phi_{X,V}=\phi_{X,V^{\prime}}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot Ff),

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\Phi_{V,W}\odot Ff)=F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\phi_{X,V^{\prime}}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot Ff).

Then the bijectivity of
\theta
implies that the diagram (
3.24
) commutes. ∎

Remark 3.25
.

Even if the functor
F
is strong monoidal, it is not guaranteed that the natural transformation
\Phi
is an isomorphism. A counterexample is the monoidal embedding
{\mathbf{P}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
described in Remark
2.28
: the formulae (
2.80
) and (
2.81
) imply that the components
\Phi_{x,y}
are not isomorphisms if
0<x<y
.

3.3.2.

Translation of representations.

For a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
define morphisms
\mu_{FX}\colon FX\odot FX\to FX
and
\eta_{FX}\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FX
as the compositions

\displaystyle FX\odot FX\xrightarrow{\phi_{X,X}}F(X\otimes X)\xrightarrow{F\mu_{X}}FX

and

\displaystyle I_{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\varphi}FI_{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{F\eta_{X}}FX

(3.101)

respectively.
Any lax monoidal functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
induces the functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})

(3.102)

which translates
{\mathbb{M}}
to the monoid
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}=(FX,\mu_{FX},\eta_{FX})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
.
If
F
is faithful, then so is
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)
. If
F
is strong monoidal and fully faithful, then
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)
is fully faithful as well.

For any object
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
the monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
is translated to the monoid
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)
, which is the object
F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\in{\mathbf{D}}
with the multiplication
F(c_{V})\cdot\phi_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V),\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)}
and unit
F(u_{V})\cdot\varphi
.

Substitution
W=V\in{\mathbf{P}}
to (
3.91
) gives the morphism

\displaystyle\Phi_{V,V}\colon F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)

(3.103)

in
{\mathbf{D}}
, where the end-object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)\in{\mathbf{D}}
is defined due to
FV\in{\mathbf{Q}}
.
The first sentence of the following statement is a particular case of the points (2) and (3) of
[
Bor2
, Prop. 6.4.5]
generalised to the relative case.

Proposition 3.26
.

The morphism (
3.103
) is a morphism in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
from the monoid
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)
to
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV),c_{FV},u_{FV}\big)
. In particular, if (
3.103
) is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
, then it gives an isomorphism of monoids
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)
.

Proof.
The first sentence can be reformulated as the commutativity of the diagrams

\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\odot F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\phi}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V,V}\odot\Phi_{V,V}}
\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 16.38895ptF(c_{V})}
\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V,V}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)\odot\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{FV}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)}

\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{u_{FV}}
\textstyle{FI_{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(u_{V})\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V,V}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)}

By using the formulae (
2.35
), (
3.96
),
\phi\cdot(\varphi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FV})=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FV}
and
\theta(u_{V})=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}
we obtain

\theta\big(\Phi_{V,V}\cdot F(u_{V})\cdot\varphi\big)=\theta\big(\Phi_{V,V}\cdot F(u_{V})\big)\cdot(\varphi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FV})=F\big(\theta(u_{V})\big)\big)\cdot\phi\cdot(\varphi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FV})=\\
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FV}=\theta(u_{FV}).

This implies the commutativity of the second diagram. The commutativity of the first one is checked in the same manner:

\theta\big(\Phi_{V,V}\cdot F(c_{V})\cdot\phi\big)=\theta\big(\Phi_{V,V}\cdot F(c_{V})\big)\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=F\big(\theta(c_{V})\big)\cdot\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\\
F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot F(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\phi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)=F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\phi\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\big)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)=\\
\theta(\Phi_{V,V})\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\theta(\Phi_{V,V})\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\Phi_{V,V}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\Phi_{V,V}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\\
