\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot(\Phi_{V,V}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\Phi_{V,V}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\theta(c_{FV})\cdot(\Phi_{V,V}\odot\Phi_{V,V}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\theta\big(c_{FV}\cdot(\Phi_{V,V}\odot\Phi_{V,V})\big),

where we also used
\theta(c_{V})=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)
,
\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\phi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)
, the formula (
2.34
) and the naturality of
\phi
. The second sentence of Prop.
3.26
follows from the fact that the forgetful functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to{\mathbf{C}}
reflects isomorphisms (see e.g.
[
S21
, Prop. 2.1]
). ∎

Let us describe how the lax monoidal functor
F
translates representations of the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
on
V
to representations of the monoid
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}
on
FV
.

Theorem 3.27
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}
. Any lax monoidal functor
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
satisfying
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
induces the functor

\displaystyle F_{{\mathbf{P}},{\mathbb{M}}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}),

(3.104)

which maps
(V,\rho)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
to
(FV,\widetilde{\rho})
, where
\widetilde{\rho}\colon\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)
is the composition

\displaystyle FX\xrightarrow{F\rho}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\xrightarrow{\Phi_{V,V}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV).

(3.105)

The image of a morphism
f\colon(V,\rho)\to(W,\tau)
under the functor
F_{{\mathbf{P}},{\mathbb{M}}}
is the morphism
Ff\colon(FV,\widetilde{\rho})\to(FW,\widetilde{\tau})
, where
(W,\tau)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
and
\widetilde{\tau}=\Phi_{W,W}\cdot F\tau
.

Proof.
The functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)
maps the monoid morphism
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
to the monoid morphism
F\rho\colon\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)
. Hence Prop.
3.26
implies that (
3.105
) is a monoid morphism
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)
. The commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{FX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\rho}
\scriptstyle{F\tau}
\textstyle{F\big([V,V]\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,f)\big)}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V,V}}
\textstyle{[FV,FV]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,Ff)}
\textstyle{F\big([W,W]\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\big)}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{W,W}}
\textstyle{F\big([V,W]\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V,W}}
\textstyle{[FW,FW]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Ff,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)}
\textstyle{[FV,FW]}

follows from the commutativity of (
3.1
) and the naturality of
\Phi
(Prop.
3.24
). ∎

3.3.3.

Translation of corepresentations.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be symmetric relatively coclosed monoidal categories with the parametrising subcategories
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}
respectively. Let
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be a colax monoidal functor satisfying
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
.

By considering the relatively closed monoidal categories
({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
and
({\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}},\odot)
with the parametrising subcategories
{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and by using the identification functors (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) we can apply the results of pp.
3.3
and
3.3
to the lax monoidal functor
F^{\mathrm{op}}\colon({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}},\odot)
.

The colax monoidal functor
F
induces the functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})

(3.112)

which translates a comonoid
(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
to
(FX,\Delta_{FX},\varepsilon_{FX})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
, where

\displaystyle\Delta_{FX}=\phi_{X,X}\cdot F\Delta_{X}\colon FX\to FX\odot FX,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{FX}=\varphi\cdot F\varepsilon_{X}\colon FX\to I_{\mathbf{D}}.

(3.113)

The monoidal structure of
F
defines the natural transformation
\Phi
with components

\displaystyle\Phi_{V,W}=\vartheta^{-1}\big(\phi_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W),V}\cdot F(\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,W})\big)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(FV,FW)\to F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big).

(3.114)

Some of these components give monoid morphisms:

\displaystyle\Phi_{V,V}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(FV)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\big).

(3.115)

If
\Phi_{V,V}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\to F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\big)
is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
, then (
3.115
) is an isomorphism of comonoids
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(FV)\cong\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\big)
.

Theorem 3.28
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
be coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}
. Let
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be a colax monoidal functor such that
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
. Let
{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{O}}
. Then
F
induces the functor

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{O}}_{{\mathbf{P}}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}),

(3.116)

which maps
(V,\omega)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})
to
(FV,\widetilde{\omega})
, where
\widetilde{\omega}
is the composition

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(FV)\xrightarrow{\Phi_{V,V}}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\big)\xrightarrow{F\omega}FX.

(3.117)

A morphism
f\colon(V,\omega)\to(W,\nu)
is mapped to the morphism
Ff\colon(FV,\widetilde{\omega})\to(FW,\widetilde{\nu})
, where
W\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
\nu
is a corepresentation of
{\mathbb{O}}
on
W
and
\widetilde{\nu}=F\nu\cdot\Phi_{W,W}
.

3.3.4.

Translation of actions.

For any monoid
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
a lax monoidal functor
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
induces a functor

\displaystyle F_{\mathbb{M}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}),

(3.118)

where
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}
. Each action
a\colon X\otimes V\to V
of
{\mathbb{M}}
is translated to the action
\widetilde{a}\colon FX\odot FV\to FV
of
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
defined as the composition

\displaystyle FX\odot FV\xrightarrow{\phi_{X,V}}F(X\otimes V)\xrightarrow{Fa}FV,

(3.119)

i.e.
(V,a)\mapsto(FV,\widetilde{a})
and
f\mapsto Ff
(see e.g.
[
S21
, § 2.4.11]
for details).

Dually, a colax monoidal functor
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
induces the functor

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{O}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})

(3.120)

for comonoids
{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
. It translates a coaction
\delta\colon V\to X\otimes V
to the coaction

\displaystyle FV\xrightarrow{F\delta}F(X\otimes V)\xrightarrow{\phi_{X,V}}FX\odot FV.

(3.121)

In the situation defined in p.
3.3
we have subcategories
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\subset\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
and
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}})\subset\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}})
. Since
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
, the restriction of the functor (
3.118
) gives a functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}})
.

Proposition 3.29
.

The restrictions of the functors (
3.118
) and (
3.120
) coincide with (
3.104
) and (
3.116
) respectively. In other words the functor diagrams

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{{\mathbf{P}},{\mathbb{M}}}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{\mathbb{M}}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}})}

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathbf{P}}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\mathbb{O}}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})}

commute, where the vertical arrows ‘
\hookrightarrow
’ are the embedding functors (
3.22
), (
3.24
).

Proof.
Repeats the proof of
[
S21
, Prop. 3.11]
. ∎

Remark 3.30
.

Theorems
3.27
and
3.28
follows from Prop.
3.29
and the construction of the functors (
3.118
) and (
3.120
) (see
[
S21
, § 3.3.3]
).
By taking the representation (
3.25
) as
\rho
in Theorem
3.27
we conclude that
\Phi_{V,V}
is a monoid morphism, so Prop.
3.26
is also a consequence the construction of (
3.118
).

3.3.5.

Translation under a contravariant functor.

We call a contravariant functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}

lax/colax/strong monoidal
from
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
to
({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
iff the corresponding covariant functor
\bar{F}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is equipped with the structure of a lax/colax/strong monoidal functor
(\bar{F},\varphi,\phi)\colon({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
. Equivalently, it means that the functor
\bar{F}^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a colax/lax/strong monoidal. Let both monoidal categories
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be symmetric. The monoidal functor
F
is called
symmetric
iff
\bar{F}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
(or, equivalently,
\bar{F}^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}
) is symmetric.

The results written for covariant monoidal functors can be reformulated for contravariant monoidal functors. We consider only colax monoidal functors applying the results of pp.
3.3
and
3.3
to the categories
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}
or, equivalently, the results of p.
3.3
to
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
.
The case of contravariant lax monoidal functor is completely analogous, while a contravariant strong monoidal functor is a particular case of a contravariant colax monoidal functor.

Suppose that the symmetric monoidal categories
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
are relatively closed and coclosed respectively. Let
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a contravariant colax monoidal functor, where
\varphi\colon FI_{\mathbf{C}}\to I_{\mathbf{D}}
and
\phi_{X,Y}\colon F(X\otimes Y)\to FX\odot FY
are structure morphisms for the colax monoidal functor
\bar{F}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
. The contravariant functor
F
induces a contravariant functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})

(3.130)

corresponding to the composition of the identification (
3.19
) with the functor (
3.112
) for covariant functor
\bar{F}
.
A monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
is translated under (
3.130
) to the comonoid
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}=(FX,\Delta_{FX},\varepsilon_{FX})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
, where

\Delta_{FX}=\phi_{X,X}\cdot F\mu_{X}
,
\varepsilon_{FX}=\varphi\cdot F\eta_{X}
.

The natural transformation
\Phi
for the contravariant case has the components

\displaystyle\Phi_{V,W}=\vartheta^{-1}\big(\phi_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W),V}\cdot F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W})\big)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(FV,FW)\to F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big),

(3.131)

V,W,\in{\mathbf{P}}
.
It gives the morphisms of comonoids
\Phi_{V,V}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(FV)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)
. If
\Phi_{V,V}
is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
, then it is an isomorphism in
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
and
{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}})
be a monoid and a comonoid related via (
3.19
).
Theorems
3.27
and
3.28
for
\bar{F}^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\bar{F}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
give the covariant functors
(\bar{F}^{\mathrm{op}})_{{\mathbf{P}},{\mathbb{M}}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}})
and
(\bar{F})^{\mathbb{O}}_{{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}}({\mathbb{O}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{Q}}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
respectively, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(\bar{F}^{\mathrm{op}}){\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}})
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(\bar{F}){\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
. By using (
3.20
) we obtain the following statement.

Theorem 3.31
.

Let the monoidal categories
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be closed and coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}
respectively. Let
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. Any contravariant colax monoidal functor
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
such that
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
induces the contravariant functor

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{M}}_{{\mathbf{P}}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}),

(3.132)

where
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}
. It maps an object
(V,\rho)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
to
(FV,\widetilde{\omega})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
, where
\widetilde{\omega}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(FV)\to\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is the corepresentation defined as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(FV)\xrightarrow{\Phi_{V,V}}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\xrightarrow{F\rho}FX.

(3.133)

A morphism
f\colon(V,\rho)\to(W,\tau)
is mapped to the morphism
Ff\colon(FV,\widetilde{\omega})\to(FW,\widetilde{\nu})
, where
(W,\tau)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
and
\widetilde{\nu}=F\tau\cdot\Phi_{W,W}
.

3.3.6.

Translation of tensor product of monoids.

We denote the symmetric structure of the both symmetric monoidal categories
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
by the same letter
\sigma
.

Lemma 3.32
.

Let
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be a lax monoidal functor. Then for any objects
X,Y,Z,W\in{\mathbf{C}}
the diagram

\textstyle{FX\odot FY\odot FZ\odot FW\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi_{Y,Z}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X,Y}\odot\phi_{Z,W}}
\textstyle{FX\odot F(Y\otimes Z)\odot FW\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\phi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y)\odot F(Z\otimes W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X\otimes Y,Z\otimes W}}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y\otimes Z\otimes W)}

is commutative. If
F
is symmetric, then we also have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{FX\odot FY\odot FZ\odot FW\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\scriptstyle{\phi\odot\phi}
\textstyle{FX\odot FZ\odot FY\odot FW\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi\odot\phi}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Z)\odot F(Y\otimes W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y)\odot F(Z\otimes W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y\otimes Z\otimes W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(\sigma^{(23)})}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Z\otimes Y\otimes W)}

Proof.
Due to the identity
\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\phi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)
we obtain
\phi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\phi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)=\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)=\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\phi)
. Then, by using (
3.32
) one yields
\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\phi)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}=\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot(\phi\cdot\sigma)\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\big)=\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot(F\sigma\cdot\phi)\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\big)
. Due to the naturality of
\phi
this is equal to
\phi\cdot\big(F(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\sigma)\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\big)\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=F(\sigma^{(23)})\cdot\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=F(\sigma^{(23)})\cdot\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\phi)
. ∎

Note that
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
and
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
are both symmetric monoidal categories (see p.
3.2
). Let us describe a monoidal structure of
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
induced by the monoidal structure of
F
.

Proposition 3.33
.

If
F
is a symmetric lax/strong monoidal functor, then
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)
is also lax/strong monoidal and also symmetric. The monoidal structure is given by the same morphisms
\varphi\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FI_{\mathbf{C}}
and
\phi_{X,Y}\colon FX\odot FY\to F(X\otimes Y)
regarded as morphisms
\varphi\colon I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})}\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)(I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})})
and
\phi_{{\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}}=\phi_{X,Y}\colon\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\odot\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)({\mathbb{M}}\otimes{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime})
, where
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
,
{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}=(Y,\mu_{Y},\eta_{Y})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}
.

Dually, if
F
is a symmetric colax/strong monoidal functor, then
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)
is also colax/strong monoidal and also symmetric. Its monoidal structure is given by the morphisms
\varphi\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FI_{\mathbf{C}}
and
\phi_{X,Y}\colon F(X\otimes Y)\to FX\odot FY
regarded as morphisms between comonoids.

Proof.
We need to check that
\phi_{X,Y}\colon FX\odot FY\to F(X\otimes Y)
is a morphism from the monoid
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\odot\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}=\big(FX\odot FY,(\mu_{FX}\odot\mu_{FY})\cdot\sigma^{(23)},\eta_{FX}\odot\eta_{FY}\big)
to the monoid
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)({\mathbb{M}}\otimes{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime})=\big(F(X\otimes Y),F(\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{Y})\cdot F(\sigma)\cdot\phi,F(\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y})\cdot\varphi\big)
, where
\mu_{FX}=F\mu_{X}\cdot\phi
,
\eta_{FX}=F\eta_{X}\cdot\varphi
. This amounts to the commutative diagrams

\textstyle{FX\odot FY\odot FX\odot FY\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt(\phi\odot\phi)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}}
\scriptstyle{\phi\odot\phi}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes X)\odot(Y\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895ptF\mu_{X}\odot F\mu_{Y}}
\textstyle{FX\odot FY\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y)\odot F(X\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447ptF(\sigma^{(23)})\cdot\phi}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes X\otimes Y\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895ptF(\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{Y})}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y)}

\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{D}}\odot I_{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi\odot\varphi}
\textstyle{FI_{\mathbf{C}}\odot FI_{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\eta_{X}\odot F\eta_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}}}}
\textstyle{FX\odot FY\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{FI_{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y})}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y)}

The commutativity of (
3.3
) follows from Lemma
3.32
(for
Z=X
,
W=Y
) and the naturality of
\phi
. The diagram (
3.3
) is obtained by using the formula
\phi_{I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}}}\cdot(\varphi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
. The same formula implies that
\varphi
is a morphism from the monoid
I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})}=(I_{{\mathbf{D}}},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
to
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})}=(FI_{\mathbf{C}},\phi_{I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}}},\varphi)
. The conditions on the monoidal structure of
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)
defined in this way as well as the agreement with the symmetric structure are valid, since they are valid for the structure of
F
. If
F
is strong monoidal, then
\varphi
and
\phi_{X,Y}
are isomorphisms in
{\mathbf{D}}
. They are isomorphisms in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
, since the forgetful functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})\to{\mathbf{D}}
reflects isomorphisms. ∎

3.3.7.

Translation of bimonoids.

Let
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be a (covariant) symmetric strong monoidal functor. Let
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
be a bimonoid in
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
. By definition it means that
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
and
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
are a monoid and a comonoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
such that the comonoid structure gives the morphisms
\Delta_{X}\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}\otimes{\mathbb{M}}
and
\varepsilon_{X}\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})}
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. The formulae (
3.101
) give the monoid
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=(FX,\mu_{FX},\eta_{FX})=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}
in
{\mathbf{D}}
.
By considering
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)
as a colax monoidal functor with the structure morphisms
\varphi^{-1}\colon FI_{\mathbf{C}}\to I_{\mathbf{D}}
and
\phi^{-1}_{X,Y}\colon F(X\otimes Y)\to FX\odot FY
we obtain the comonoid
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=(FX,\Delta_{FX},\varepsilon_{FX})=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{O}}
in
{\mathbf{D}}
, where
\Delta_{FX}
and
\varepsilon_{FX}
are defined by the formulae (
3.113
) but with
\varphi^{-1}
and
\phi^{-1}
instead of
\varphi
and
\phi
, i.e.

\displaystyle\Delta_{FX}\colon FX\xrightarrow{F\Delta_{X}}F(X\otimes X)\xrightarrow{\phi^{-1}_{X,X}}FX\odot FX,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{FX}\colon FX\xrightarrow{F\varepsilon_{X}}FI_{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{\varphi^{-1}}I_{\mathbf{D}}.

(3.146)

By applying Prop.
3.33
to the strong monoidal functor
(F,\varphi,\phi)
we see that
\phi_{X,X}
and
\varphi
are monoid isomorphisms with the inverses
\phi^{-1}_{X,X}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)({\mathbb{M}}\otimes{\mathbb{M}})\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\odot\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
and
\varphi^{-1}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})}\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})}
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}
. Hence the compositions (
3.146
) give the monoid morphisms
\Delta_{FX}\colon\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\to\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\odot\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
and
\varepsilon_{FX}\colon\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\to I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})}
, so we get a bimonoid
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}=(FX,\mu_{FX},\eta_{FX},\Delta_{FX},\varepsilon_{FX})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
. We have proved the following fact.

Proposition 3.34
.

A (covariant) symmetric strong monoidal functor
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
translates bimonoids to bimonoids. Namely, it induces the symmetric strong monoidal functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}},\odot)

(3.147)

agreed with the functors (
3.102
) and (
3.112
). It maps
{\mathbb{B}}
to the bimonoid
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{B}}
defined by the formulae (
3.101
), (
3.146
).

A contravariant symmetric strong monoidal functor
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
gives the contravariant functor

\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}},\odot)

corresponding to covariant functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(\bar{F})\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
. It translates
{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
to
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{B}}=(FX,\mu_{FX},\eta_{FX},\Delta_{FX},\varepsilon_{FX})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
, where

\displaystyle\mu_{FX}\colon FX\odot FX\xrightarrow{\phi_{X,X}}F(X\otimes X)\xrightarrow{F\Delta_{X}}FX,

\displaystyle\eta_{FX}\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\varphi}FI_{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{F\varepsilon_{X}}FX,

(3.148)

\displaystyle\Delta_{FX}\colon FX\xrightarrow{F\mu_{X}}F(X\otimes X)\xrightarrow{\phi_{X,X}^{-1}}FX\odot FX,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{FX}\colon FX\xrightarrow{F\eta_{X}}FI_{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{\varphi^{-1}}I_{\mathbf{D}}.

(3.149)

3.3.8.

Translation of tensor product of representations.

Suppose that the parametrising subcategories
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
are monoidal subcategories of the symmetric monoidal categories
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
respectively and that
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
for a covariant strong monoidal functor
F
. Let
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{B}}
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
are both relatively closed, then we have the functors (
3.104
). Denote such functor for a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
by

\displaystyle F_{{\mathbf{P}},{\mathbb{B}}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}).

(3.150)

In the case, when
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
are both relatively coclosed, the functor (
3.116
) for the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
and the colax monoidal functor
F=(F,\varphi^{-1},\phi^{-1})
is defined and has the form

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{B}}_{{\mathbf{P}}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}).

(3.151)

If
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
are relatively closed and coclosed respectively and
F
is a contravariant symmetric strong monoidal functor, then we get the contravariant functor

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{B}}_{{\mathbf{P}}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widehat{\mathbb{B}}),

(3.152)

where
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}
is defined by (
3.148
),
(
3.149
)
.

Recall that all the categories appeared in (
3.150
), (
3.151
) and (
3.152
) are monoidal categories (Theorems
3.18
,
3.20
).

Theorem 3.35
.

The functors (
3.150
), (
3.151
) and (
3.152
) are strong monoidal functors (the corresponding conditions are supposed: the symmetric monoidal categories
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
should be relatively closed/coclosed, the symmetric strong monoidal
F
should be covariant/contravariant). The monoidal structure of the functors (
3.150
), (
3.151
) and (
3.152
) is inherited from
F
.

Proof.
Let
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
be representations of
{\mathbb{B}}
on the objects
V
and
V^{\prime}
. Their tensor product
\rho^{\prime\prime}=\pi\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})\cdot\Delta_{X}
is a representation of
{\mathbb{B}}
on
V^{\prime\prime}=V\otimes V^{\prime}
. The functor
F
translates the objects
(V,\rho)
,
(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
and
(V^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime\prime})
of
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
to the objects
(FV,\tau)
,
(FV^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
and
(FV^{\prime\prime},\tau^{\prime\prime})
of
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}})
, where
\tau=\Phi_{V,V}\cdot F\rho
,
\tau^{\prime}=\Phi_{V^{\prime},V^{\prime}}\cdot F\rho^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}=\Phi_{V^{\prime\prime},V^{\prime\prime}}\cdot F\rho^{\prime\prime}
. The tensor product of
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
is the representation
\lambda=\pi\cdot(\tau\odot\tau^{\prime})\cdot\Delta_{FX}
on
FV\odot FV^{\prime}
. We need to show that
\phi_{V,V^{\prime}}\colon FV\odot FV^{\prime}\to F(V\otimes V^{\prime})
is a morphism between representations
\lambda
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}
. According to Definition
3.1
this amounts to the commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda\hskip 16.38895pt}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV\odot FV^{\prime},FV\odot FV^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\phi)}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV^{\prime\prime},FV^{\prime\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\phi,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV\odot FV^{\prime},FV^{\prime\prime})}

The representations in this diagram can be written in the form

\displaystyle\lambda=\pi\cdot(\tau\odot\tau^{\prime})\cdot\Delta_{FX}=\pi\cdot(\Phi\odot\Phi)\cdot(F\rho\odot F\rho^{\prime})\cdot\phi^{-1}\cdot F\Delta_{X}=

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\pi\cdot(\Phi\odot\Phi)\cdot\phi^{-1}\cdot F(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})\cdot F\Delta_{X},

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime\prime}=\Phi\cdot F\rho^{\prime\prime}=\Phi\cdot F\pi\cdot F(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})\cdot F\Delta_{X},

so we need to check the equality

\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\phi)\cdot\pi\cdot(\Phi\odot\Phi)=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\phi,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\Phi\cdot F\pi\cdot\phi
of two morphisms
F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\big)\odot F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},V^{\prime})\big)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV\odot FV^{\prime},FV^{\prime\prime})
. By using the formulae (
2.35
), (
2.67
) and the commutative diagrams (
3.2
), (
3.3
) we obtain their images under
\theta
:

\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\phi)\big)\cdot(\pi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\Phi\odot\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\phi\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\pi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\Phi\odot\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\\
\phi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\odot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\cdot(\Phi\odot\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\phi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\odot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot(\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}=\\
\phi\cdot\big(F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\odot F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\big)\cdot(\phi\odot\phi)\cdot\sigma^{(23)}=F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\phi)\cdot\sigma^{(23)},

(3.157)

\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\phi,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\big)\cdot(\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(F\pi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\\
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi)\cdot(\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(F\pi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\phi\cdot(F\pi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\phi\odot\phi)=\\
F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot F(\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\phi)=F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot F(\sigma^{(23)})\cdot\phi\cdot(\phi\odot\phi).

(3.158)

The right hand sides of (
3.157
) and (
3.158
) coincide by virtue of Lemma
3.32
, so the diagram (
3.3
) is commutative.

The unit object
I_{\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})}=(I_{\mathbf{C}},\varepsilon_{X})
is translated to the object
(FI_{\mathbf{C}},\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{X})
, where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{X}\colon FX\xrightarrow{F\varepsilon_{X}}FI_{\mathbf{C}}=F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{C}})\big)\xrightarrow{\Phi_{I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}}}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FI_{\mathbf{C}})

(3.159)

is the corresponding representation of
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}
on
FI_{\mathbf{C}}
. Let us prove that
\varphi\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FI_{\mathbf{C}}
gives the morphisms between the representations
\varepsilon_{FX}=\varphi^{-1}\cdot F\varepsilon_{X}\colon FX\to I_{D}=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{D}})
and
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{X}
. Due to Prop.
2.25
the morphism
I_{\mathbf{D}}=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{D}},I_{\mathbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\varphi)}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{D}},FI_{\mathbf{C}})=FI_{\mathbf{C}}
coincides with
\varphi
, so we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\varphi)\cdot\varepsilon_{FX}=\varphi\cdot\varphi^{-1}\cdot F\varepsilon_{X}=F\varepsilon_{X},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\varphi,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{X}=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\varphi,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot\Phi\cdot F\varepsilon_{X}.

Hence we need only to check that the morphism

\displaystyle F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}})\big)\xrightarrow{\Phi}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FI_{\mathbf{C}},FI_{\mathbf{C}})\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\varphi,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{D}},FI_{\mathbf{C}})

(3.160)

coincides with the identification
F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}})\big)=FI_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{D}},FI_{\mathbf{C}})
. Since (
2.69
) is the identification, the morphism (
3.160
) coincides with the image of (
3.160
) itself under
\theta
. Due to (
2.35
) and (
2.67
) this image equals

\theta(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\varphi,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits))\cdot(\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\varphi)\cdot(\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits\cdot(\Phi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\varphi)=\\
F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\cdot\phi\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\varphi)\colon F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}})\big)\odot I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FI_{\mathbf{C}}.

(3.161)

Since
F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits)\colon F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}})\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}}\big)\to FI_{\mathbf{C}}
and

F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}})\big)\odot I_{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\varphi}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}})\big)\odot FI_{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{\phi}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(I_{\mathbf{C}},I_{\mathbf{C}})\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}}\big)

are both the identification morphisms, their composition (
3.161
) is the identification as well.

By virtue of Prop.
3.2
the morphisms of representations
\phi_{V,V^{\prime}}\colon(FV\odot FV^{\prime},\lambda)\to(FV^{\prime\prime},\tau^{\prime\prime})
and
\varphi\colon(I_{\mathbf{D}},\varepsilon_{FX})\to(FX,\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{X})
are isomorphisms in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
. ∎

Remark 3.36
.

Recall that any symmetric strong monoidal functor
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)
induces strong monoidal functors

\displaystyle F_{\mathbb{B}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}),

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{B}}\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}})

(3.162)

with the (lax) monoidal structure
(\varphi,\phi)
. The functors (
3.150
) and (
3.151
) are restrictions of (
3.162
) as monoidal functors (in the sense of Prop.
3.29
and Remark
3.22
). These facts can be used for an alternative proof of Theorem
3.35
.

4
Examples

The general theory of representations described above was motivated by two types of representations. First, this theory allows to unify the classical representations on vector spaces such as representations of groups and algebras. Second example is quantum representations on quadratic algebras defined in
[
S21
]
.

Let us fix a basic field
{\mathbb{K}}
of characteristic
\mathop{\mathrm{char}}\nolimits{\mathbb{K}}\neq 2
. We suppose that any vector space or algebra is over
{\mathbb{K}}
unless we explicitly specify another field.

4.1
Representations on vector spaces

The model example of the general representation theory is the representations of algebras. This is the case of the closed monoidal category of vector spaces. To describe the representations of groups we introduce a bigger relatively closed monoidal category which contains sets as well as vector spaces as monoidal subcategories. This gives the unification of representations of groups and algebras. This case is generalised to groups with a geometric structure such as Lie groups, algebraic groups etc (the algebraic case was described in
[
S21
, § 4.3]
).

4.1.1.

Representations of algebras.

The category of vector spaces
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
with the standard tensor product
\otimes
is a closed symmetric monoidal category with the unit object
{\mathbb{K}}
. The monoids in
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
are algebras:
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)=({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)
. For
V,W\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
the object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
is a vector space of all the linear operators
V\to W
, while
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
is the algebra of operators on
V
. A morphism
\rho\colon{\mathfrak{A}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)={\mathbf{Alg}}
is a representation of an algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
on
V\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
in the usual sense.

4.1.2.

Semi-linear sets.

The category of sets
{\mathbf{Set}}
is the category with finite products. The usual monoids and groups are monoids and groups in
{\mathbf{Set}}
, i.e. monoids and Hopf monoids in
({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
. Notice that the monoidal category
({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
is closed with the hom-objects
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\in{\mathbf{Set}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(X)=\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(X)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
, where
X,Y\in{\mathbf{Set}}
.

To consider representations of monoids and groups on vector spaces in frame of the general representation theory constructed in Section
3
we define the category
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
. Its objects are pairs
(X,V)
, where
X\in{\mathbf{Set}}
and
V\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
; denote such object by
X\times V
. Define morphisms by the formula

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{SLSet}}(X\times V,Y\times W)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Set}}(X,Y)\times\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Set}}\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Vect}}(V,W)\big).

(4.1)

A morphism
X\times V\to Y\times W
is presented by a collection
\big(\varphi,(f_{x})_{x\in X}\big)
, where
\varphi\colon X\to Y
is an arbitrary map and
f_{x}\colon V\to W
are linear maps indexed by
x\in X
.

We can consider the object
X\times V\in{\mathbf{SLSet}}
as the Cartesian product of the sets
X
and
V
. A morphism
F=(\varphi,f_{x})
in
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
can be considered as the map
F\colon X\times V\to Y\times W
given by the formula

\displaystyle F(x,v)=\big(\varphi(x),f_{x}(v)\big).

(4.2)

Such maps are linear only in the second variable, so we call them
semi-linear maps
.
If
(\psi,g_{y})\colon Y\times W\to Z\times U
is another morphism in
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
for some
Y\in{\mathbf{Set}}
and
U\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
, then the composition
X\times V\xrightarrow{(\varphi,f_{x})}Y\times W\xrightarrow{(\psi,g_{y})}Z\times U
has the form

\displaystyle(\psi,g_{y})\cdot(\varphi,f_{x})=(\psi\cdot\varphi,g_{\varphi(x)}\cdot f_{x}).

(4.3)

This formula defines the composition in
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
. Due to the associativity of the composition of maps we indeed defined a category
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
. It is a concrete category with the forgetful functor
{\mathbf{SLSet}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
defined by (
4.2
). We call its objects
semi-linear spaces
or
semi-linear sets
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{Set}}
and
V,V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
. We introduce the tensor product of the semi-linear sets
X\times V
and
X^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}
by the formula

\displaystyle(X\times V)\otimes(X^{\prime}\times V^{\prime})=(X\times X^{\prime})\times(V\otimes V^{\prime}).

(4.4)

The tensor product on morphisms is defined as

\displaystyle\big(\varphi,(f_{x})_{x\in X}\big)\otimes\big(\varphi^{\prime},(f^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}})_{x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}}\big)=\big(\varphi\times\varphi^{\prime},(f_{x}\otimes f^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}})_{(x,x^{\prime})\in X\times X^{\prime}}\big),

(4.5)

where
\varphi\colon X\to Y
,
f_{x}\colon V\to W
,
\varphi^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
,
f^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}}\colon V^{\prime}\to W^{\prime}
.
This gives a symmetric monoidal category
({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
with the unit object
\{0\}\times{\mathbb{K}}
, where
\{0\}\in{\mathbf{Set}}
and
{\mathbb{K}}\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
are unit objects of
({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
and
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
respectively. There is one more useful ‘forgetful’ functor
{\mathbf{SLSet}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
,
X\times V\mapsto X
. It is a strict monoidal functor
({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
and hence it induces
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
.

An object
{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
is a semi-linear set
X\times V
with the semi-linear maps

\displaystyle\mu_{X\times V}=\big(\mu_{X},(f_{x,y})_{(x,y)\in X\times X}\big)\colon(X\times X)\times(V\otimes V)\to X\times V,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\eta_{X\times V}=(\eta_{X},\eta_{V})\colon\{0\}\times{\mathbb{K}}\to X\times V

(4.7)

satisfying the following conditions. The triple
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
should be a monoid in
({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
, denote
e=\eta_{X}(0)\in X
and
xy=\mu_{X}(x,y)
. The linear maps
f_{x,y}\colon V\otimes V\to V
and
\eta_{V}\colon{\mathbb{K}}\to V
are subjected to
f_{x,yz}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f_{y,z})=f_{xy,z}\cdot(f_{x,y}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
and
f_{e,x}\cdot(\eta_{V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits=f_{x,e}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\eta_{V})
.
We call
{\mathbb{S}}=(X\times V,\mu_{X\times V},\eta_{X\times V})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
a
semi-linear
monoid.

The fully faithful strict monoidal functor

\displaystyle({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)\hookrightarrow({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes),

\displaystyle V\mapsto\{0\}\times V,

(4.8)

embeds the category
{\mathbf{Vect}}
into
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
as a full monoidal subcategory of the monoidal category
({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
.

We embed
{\mathbf{Set}}
into
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
by the faithful strict monoidal functor

\displaystyle({\mathbf{Set}},\times)\hookrightarrow({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes),

\displaystyle X\mapsto X\times{\mathbb{K}}.

(4.9)

This embedding functor is not full. It allows us to regard the category
{\mathbf{Set}}
as a (not full) monoidal subcategory of
({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
.

Remark 4.1
.

The category
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
is also a category with finite products and
({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
can be considered as a monoidal subcategory of
({\mathbf{SLSet}},\times)
via another embedding
{\mathbf{Set}}\to{\mathbf{SLSet}}
,
X\mapsto X\times 0
. The embedding
V\mapsto\{0\}\times V
does not respect the tensor product
\otimes
of vector spaces, it is the strict monoidal functor
({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{SLSet}},\times)
. However, these embeddings will not give the classical representations on vector spaces.

4.1.3.

Representations of usual monoids and groups.

A classical notion of the representation of a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
is a monoid homomorphism
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
is the monoid of the linear operators on
V
. When
{\mathbb{M}}
is a group, the image of
\rho
lies in the group
GL(V)\subset\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
consisting of the invertible operators on
V
, so the notion of the representation of a monoid on a linear space includes the linear representations of groups.

The
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits
-functor (
3.102
) induced by the fully faithful strict monoidal functor (
4.8
) is the category embedding
{\mathbf{Alg}}\hookrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLSet}},\times)
. It identifies an algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}=({\mathfrak{A}},\mu_{V},\eta_{V})\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
with the semi-linear monoid
\{0\}\times{\mathfrak{A}}
; in this case
X=\{0\}
, the maps
\mu_{X}
,
\eta_{X}
are trivial and
f_{0,0,0}=\mu_{V}\colon V\otimes V\to V
,
\eta_{V}\colon V\to{\mathbb{K}}
are the structure morphisms of the algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}
.

The functor (
4.9
) induces the embedding
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)\hookrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
. The monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
is identified with the semi-linear monoid

\displaystyle{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{M}}=\Big(X\times{\mathbb{K}},\big(\mu_{X},(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{K}})\big),(\eta_{V},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{K}})\Big)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes).

(4.10)

We will see that the representations of a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
on
V
are exactly the representations of
{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{M}}
on the object
V
identified with
\{0\}\times V\in{\mathbf{SLSet}}
via the embedding (
4.8
).

First, we need to check that
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
is closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{Vect}}
. We, however, can prove that it is closed (relative to the whole
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
). For two semi-linear sets
X\times V,Y\times W\in{\mathbf{SLSet}}
define an object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X\times V,Y\times W)\in{\mathbf{SLSet}}
as the Cartesian product (
4.1
), where
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)
is equipped by the structure of a linear space as follows: the linear combination of
f,g\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
with the coefficients
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{K}}
is the map
X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
with the values

\displaystyle(\alpha f+\beta g)(x)=\alpha f(x)+\beta g(x)\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W),

\displaystyle x\in X.

(4.11)

Introduce the map
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{X\times V,Y\times W}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X\times V,Y\times W)\otimes(X\times V)\to Y\times W
, which evaluates the map
(\varphi,f_{x})\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X\times V,Y\times W)
on
(x,v)\in X\times V
. It maps an element

\displaystyle(\varphi,x,f\otimes v)\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\times X\times\Big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\big)\otimes V\Big)

(4.12)

to
\big(\varphi(x),f(x)(v)\big)\in Y\times W
. One can show that
\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X\times V,Y\times W),\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{X\times V,Y\times W}\big)
is a universal arrow from the functor
-\otimes(X\times V)\colon{\mathbf{SLSet}}\to{\mathbf{SLSet}}
to the object
Y\times W
. Then Theorem
2.13
implies that
({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
is closed with the hom-objects defined in this way.

For the case
X=Y=\{0\}
we have
\{0\}\times\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\{0\}\times V,\{0\}\times W)
(this identification is the isomorphism
\Phi_{V,W}
induced by the monoidal functor (
4.8
)), so the internal hom-object in
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
is the same as the internal hom-object in
({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
for the vector spaces embedded by (
4.8
). The internal end is the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(\{0\}\times V)=\{0\}\times\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
identified with the algebra of operators
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
.

A representation of an algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}=\{0\}\times{\mathfrak{A}}
on
V=\{0\}\times V
in the sense of Definition
3.1
is exactly the algebra morphism
\rho\colon{\mathfrak{A}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
, so it is a representation of the algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}
in the usual sense.

Consider a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
as the semi-linear monoid
{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{M}}
defined by (
4.10
). Then a representation of
{\mathbb{M}}
on
V=\{0\}\times V
is a representation
\widehat{\rho}\colon{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
. This is a morphism
X\times{\mathbb{K}}\to\{0\}\times\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
in
{\mathbf{SLSet}}
given by the trivial map
0\colon X\to\{0\}
and a collection of linear maps
f_{x}=\rho_{x}\colon{\mathbb{K}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
. Define the map
\rho\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
as
\rho(x)=\rho_{x}(1)
. Any collection
\rho_{x}
is given by such map
\rho
. The morphism
\widehat{\rho}=(0,\rho_{x})
is a representation (a morphism in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
) iff
\rho(x)\rho(y)=\rho(xy)
for any
x,y\in X
. The latter condition means exactly that
\rho
is a representation of the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
on
V
.

Thus a linear representation of a usual monoid or group
{\mathbb{M}}
is its representation in the monoidal category
({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
on an object
V
of the subcategory
{\mathbf{Vect}}\subset{\mathbf{SLSet}}
.

4.1.4.

Representations of Lie groups.

Consider the category of smooth manifolds
{\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}}
. This is a category with finite products. A monoid
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}},\times)
is a manifold
X
with a smooth multiplication
X\times X\to X
and a unity
e\in X
. If any element
x\in X
is invertible with respect to this multiplication and the map
X\to X
,
x\mapsto x^{-1}
, is smooth, then
{\mathbb{M}}
is a Lie group, so the category of Lie groups is a full subcategory of
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}},\times)
.

Suppose that
{\mathbb{K}}
is an extension of the field of real numbers:
{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{K}}
.
A representation of a Lie group or monoid
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}},\times)
on a vector space
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
is a smooth monoid homomorphism
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
, where
{\mathbf{FVect}}
is the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces and the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
is considered as a smooth manifold
V^{*}\otimes V
(this is possible for finite dimensional
V
only). Such representations can be interpreted in terms of Section
3
by introducing a smooth version of semi-linear spaces.

Define the category
{\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}}
. An object of this category is a pair
(X,V)
of
X\in{\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}}
,
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
, denoted by
X\times V
and regarded as a Cartesian product of smooth manifolds. The morphisms
F\colon X\times V\to Y\times W
are smooth maps of the form (
4.2
). The objects
X\times V\in{\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}}
are called
smooth semi-linear spaces
or
semi-linear manifolds
.

We obtain the following faithful (‘forgetful’) functors. By forgetting semi-linear structure of
X\times V
we can consider it as an abstract manifold, this gives the forgetful functor
{\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}}\to{\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}}
. By forgetting the smoothness we obtain
{\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}}\to{\mathbf{SLSet}}
. Finally we have the functor
{\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}}\to{\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}}
which omits the linear part:
X\times V\mapsto X
.

The category
{\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}}
has a monoidal structure with the tensor product defined by (
4.4
), (
4.5
). Monoids in
({\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}},\otimes)
are called
smooth semi-linear monoids
.
The formulae
X\mapsto X\times{\mathbb{K}}
and
V\mapsto\{0\}\times V
define the embeddings (also faithful and fully faithful respectively)

\displaystyle({\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}},\times)\hookrightarrow({\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}},\otimes),

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\hookrightarrow({\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}},\otimes).

(4.13)

The monoidal category
({\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}},\otimes)
is symmetric, but it is not closed in the usual sense. It is closed relative to the subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FVect}}
defined by the right embedding (
4.13
).
The functors (
4.13
) induce the embeddings

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}},\times)\hookrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}},\otimes),

\displaystyle{\mathbf{FAlg}}\hookrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}},\otimes),

(4.14)

which identifies a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}},\times)
(in particular, a Lie group
{\mathbb{M}}
) and a finite-dimensional algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}\in{\mathbf{FAlg}}
with the corresponding smooth semi-linear monoids
{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{M}}
and
\{0\}\times{\mathfrak{A}}
respectively. A representation of
{\mathbb{M}}
and of
{\mathfrak{A}}
on a vector space
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
can be interpreted as a representation of
{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{M}}
and of
\{0\}\times{\mathfrak{A}}
respectively in the same way as for the case of p.
4.1
. In particular, the linear representations of Lie groups are representations in the monoidal category
({\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}},\otimes)
.

Similarly, one can interpret the representations of a complex Lie group by using the category of complex manifolds instead of
{\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}}
. In this case we should suppose
{\mathbb{C}}\subset{\mathbb{K}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

Consider the category of vector bundles over non-fixed manifold. A morphisms of two such bundles
E
and
E^{\prime}
with projections
\pi\colon E\to X
and
\pi^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is a pair of smooth maps
F\colon E\to E^{\prime}
and
\varphi\colon X\to X^{\prime}
such that
\varphi\cdot\pi=\pi^{\prime}\cdot F
and the induced maps
F_{x}\colon\pi^{-1}(x)\to(\pi^{\prime})^{-1}\big(\varphi(x)\big)
are linear.
The category
{\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}}
coincides with the subcategory of trivial vector bundles
E=X\times V
(we use the term ‘semi-linear space’ instead of ‘trivial vector bundle’ by the reasons explained in
[
S21
, § 4.3.3]
). The role of
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
could be played by the category of all the locally trivial vector bundles with the tensor product defined via local triviality, but its subcategory
{\mathbf{SLMan}^{\infty}}
is enough to consider representations of Lie groups from the point of view of the general representation theory.

4.1.5.

Representations of affine algebraic groups.

Let us fix an infinite subfield
{\mathbb{F}}\subset{\mathbb{K}}
and consider the category
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
of the (affine) algebraic sets over
{\mathbb{F}}
.
Since
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
is a category with finite products, we have the monoidal category
({\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}},\times)
. Monoids/Hopf monoids in this monoidal category are called
(affine) algebraic monoids/groups
(over
{\mathbb{F}}
). By using these category instead of
{\mathbf{Set}}
or
{\mathbf{Man}^{\infty}}
we can interpret representations of algebraic groups and monoids on vector spaces in terms of the general representation theory. The case
{\mathbb{F}}={\mathbb{K}}
is described in details in
[
S21
, § 4.3]
.

Remind the main notions. By considering the Cartesian products
X\times V
for
X\in{\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
and
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
as algebraic sets we get the category
{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
(the index
{\mathbb{F}}
means that
X
is over
{\mathbb{F}}
, the linear part
V
is over
{\mathbb{K}}
). Its objects are called
semi-linear (affine) algebraic sets
or
(affine algebraic) semi-linear spaces
. Their morphisms are regular maps of the form (
4.2
); their tensor products are defined by the same formula (
4.4
). The objects of
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}},\times)
are called
semi-linear algebraic monoids
(over
{\mathbb{F}}
).

The monoidal categories
({\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}},\times)
and
({\mathbf{FVect}},\times)
are identified with the monoidal subcategories of
({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}},\otimes)
by
X\mapsto X\times{\mathbb{K}}
,
V\mapsto\{0\}\times V
. Then
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}},\otimes)
is closed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FVect}}
. A representation of an algebraic monoid/group or finite-dimensional algebra on
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
is exactly a representation of the corresponding semi-linear algebraic monoid on
V
.

4.2
Quantum representations on quadratic algebras

Now let us remind the main points of Quantum Representation Theory developed in
[
S21
]
and apply the general representation theory to this case. Note that the quantum representations can be considered as generalisations of the representations of algebras, algebraic groups and semi-linear algebraic monoids.

4.2.1.

Quantum linear spaces.

For two
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
-graded algebras
{\mathcal{A}}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\geqslant 0}{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
and
{\mathcal{B}}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\geqslant 0}{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
we denote
{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\geqslant 0}({\mathcal{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}_{k})
. This operation was introduced by Manin in
[
Man87
,
Man88
]
for quadratic algebras. We call it
(white) Manin product
, it defines a symmetric monoidal structure on the category of all the
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
-graded algebras
{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
. The unit object is the polynomial algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[u]=\bigoplus\limits_{k\geqslant 0}{\mathbb{K}}u^{k}
.

The finitely generated quadratic algebras over
{\mathbb{K}}
(i.e. connected) form a monoidal subcategory
({\mathbf{FQA}},\circ)\subset({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
. This subcategory is a coclosed monoidal category with the internal cohom-objects
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})={\mathcal{A}}^{!}\bullet{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
,
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
(see
[
Man88
, § 4]
,
[
S21
, § 4.4]
). Manin interpreted the opposite category
{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
as a quantum (non-commutative) version of
{\mathbf{FVect}}
and called its objects the
quantum linear spaces
. The classical finite-dimensional vector spaces are lifted to the quantum level by the contravariant colax monoidal embedding
S^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\hookrightarrow({\mathbf{FQA}},\circ)
,
V\mapsto SV^{*}
. Alternatively one can use the contravariant strong monoidal embedding
T^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\hookrightarrow({\mathbf{FQA}},\circ)
,
V\mapsto TV^{*}
(see
[
S21
]
for details).

The corepresentations of a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=({\mathcal{A}},\Delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{FQA}},\circ)
on a quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
or, equivalently, the representations of a monoid in
({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
on a quantum linear space
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
are quantum analogues for the finite-dimensional representations of finite-dimensional algebras.

The category
{\mathbf{QA}}
consisting of all the quadratic algebras over
{\mathbb{K}}
(including infinitely generated) is a monoidal subcategory of
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
. We proved in
[
S21
, § 4.4.5]
that for any
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
the functor
-\circ{\mathcal{B}}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}
has a left adjoint (relative to the whole
{\mathbf{QA}}
), hence the symmetric monoidal category
({\mathbf{QA}},\circ)
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}
due to the point (3) of Prop.
2.19
applied to the monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{QA}},\circ)
. The corepresentations of comonoids
{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{QA}},\circ)
on
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
generalise the finite-dimensional representations of infinite-dimensional algebras at least partially.

4.2.2.

Quantum semi-linear spaces.

The algebraic set can be considered as a particular case the affine scheme. Categorically this fact is interpreted via a fully faithful functor
A\colon{\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{CommAlg}}_{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, where
{\mathbf{CommAlg}}_{\mathbb{F}}
is the category of the commutative algebras over
{\mathbb{F}}
and
A(X)
is the algebra of regular
{\mathbb{F}}
-valued functions on the algebraic set
X\in{\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
. Hence
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
is contravariantly embedded into
{\mathbf{Alg}}_{\mathbb{F}}
, the category of algebras over
{\mathbb{F}}
. This is a standard way to interpret the algebras as non-commutative affine algebraic sets/varieties/schemes.

The contravariant functor
S^{*}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}
is extended to the contravariant faithful functor
S^{*}\colon{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}\to{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
as
S^{*}(X\times V)=A(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}SV^{*}=A(X)^{e}\otimes SV^{*}
, where
{\mathfrak{R}}^{e}={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}{\mathbb{K}}
is the extension of scalars. In particular, an object
X\times{\mathbb{K}}
(that is an algebraic set
X\in{\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
embedded into
{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
) is lifted to the quantum level as
A(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}{\mathbb{K}}[u]
, the latter is a graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
with components
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\cong A(X)^{e}
. The contravariant functor
S^{*}
has a colax monoidal structure
({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
. Similarly, the functor
T^{*}
is extended to a contravariant faithful strong monoidal functor
T^{*}\colon({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
,
T^{*}(X\times V)=A(X)^{e}\otimes TV^{*}
(if
{\mathbb{F}}={\mathbb{K}}
, then
S^{*}
and
T^{*}
are fully faithful).

The images of
{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
under the functors
S^{*}
and
T^{*}
lie in the category
{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
of semi-connected quadratic algebras. This is a full subcategory

{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}\subset{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
consisting of the quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
, where
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
. Moreover, these images lie in
{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
, the full subcategory of the quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
, where
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
. The objects of
{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
or at least of
{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
can be considered as quantum analogues of the semi-linear algebraic sets over
{\mathbb{K}}
(see details in
[
S21
]
).

It was shown in
[
S21
, § 4.4.6]
that for any
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
the functor
-\circ{\mathcal{B}}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}\to{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
has a left adjoint. Due to the point (3) of Prop.
2.19
the monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})\in{\mathbf{FQA}}\subset{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
for any
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
, Prop.
2.20
implies that the monoidal subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}=({\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
is also coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}
.

Corepresentations of comonoids
{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
on
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
are quantum analogues of representations of semi-linear algebraic monoids. In particular, they give a quantum version of the representations of the affine algebraic groups. A classical representation is considered as a corepresentation on a quadratic algebra via the functor (
3.132
), where
F\colon({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
is
S^{*}
or
T^{*}
. For the case of an algebraic group or monoid one need to compose these functors with the embedding
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}_{\mathbb{F}}
. In the case of the strong monoidal functor
F=T^{*}
we obtain a contravariant monoidal embedding of representations (
3.152
).

One can lift the representations of groups, monoids
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
and, more generally, semi-linear monoids
{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLSet}},\otimes)
to the quantum level by the same formulae with
A(X)
replaced by the algebra of all the functions
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{K}}
on
X\in{\mathbf{Set}}
.

4.2.3.

The case of all graded algebras.

The category
{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
is enough to lift the classical representations of algebraic monoids and (finite-dimensional) algebras to the quantum level, however, sometimes we need to consider more general
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
-graded and
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebras (see e.g.
[
S21
, § 6.2.2]
).

Let us extend the monoidal category
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
to the case of all the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebras. Denote it by
{\mathbb{Z}}
-
\mathbf{GrAlg}
. The Manin product of the algebras
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in\textbf{${\mathbb{Z}}$-GrAlg}
is the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vector space with the components
({\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}})_{k}={\mathcal{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and multiplication
(a_{k}\otimes b_{k})(a^{\prime}_{l}\otimes b^{\prime}_{l})=(a_{k}a^{\prime}_{l})\otimes(b_{k}b^{\prime}_{l})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k+l}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}_{k+l}
, where
a_{k}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
,
a^{\prime}_{l}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
,
b_{k}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
,
b^{\prime}_{l}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
.

In
[
S21
]
we defined a
quantum representation
of a
quantum algebra

{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
on
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
as a morphism
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathbb{O}}
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
. More generally, we can define it as a morphism in
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}},\circ)
from the same
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})
to a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}},\circ)
. This notion can be interpreted in terms of Section
3
as corepresentation (or as a representation in the opposite category) due to the following statement.

Theorem 4.3
.

The monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}},\circ)
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}
.

Proof.
Note that the category
{\mathbf{QA}}
is a monoidal subcategory of
({\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}},\circ)
and that
({\mathbf{QA}},\circ)
is coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}
. In order to apply Theorem
2.26
we need to check that
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{QA}}
is a coreflective subcategory of
{\mathbf{C}}={\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}}
. For a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
denote by
G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}
the subalgebra of
{\mathcal{A}}
generated by the subspace
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
. This is an
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
-graded algebra
G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}
generated by its first order component
V={\mathcal{A}}_{1}=(G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}})_{1}
, so we have the graded epimorphism
a\colon TV\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}
, where
TV\in{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
is the tensor algebra. The kernel of
a
is a graded ideal
I=\bigoplus\limits_{k\geqslant 2}I_{k}
in
TV
, where
I_{k}=I\cap V^{\otimes k}
, and we obtain the isomorphism
G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}\cong TV/I
in
{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
. Note that
TV/I
is a quotient algebra of the quadratic algebra
TV/(I_{2})\in{\mathbf{QA}}
, where
(I_{2})
is the ideal generated by
I_{2}\subset V^{\otimes 2}
. Let
G{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(I_{2})
and
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\colon G{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
be the composition

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\colon G{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(I_{2})\twoheadrightarrow TV/I\cong G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}.

(4.15)

Its first graded component is the identity morphism
(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})_{1}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\colon V\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
.
Consider a quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{B}}=TW/(S)\in{\mathbf{QA}}
and a graded homomorphism
f\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
, where
W\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
and
S
is a subspace of
W^{\otimes 2}
. Since
{\mathcal{B}}
is generated by
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}=W
, the image of
f
lies in the subalgebra
G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
, so it factors as in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}

Such morphism
f^{\prime}\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}
is unique, since the embedding
G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
is a monomorphism. Moreover, one can show that any
f^{\prime}\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to TV/I
factors as

\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{TV/(I_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TV/I}

through a unique
h\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to TV/(I_{2})
. Indeed, the first component of
TV/(I_{2})\twoheadrightarrow TV/I
is
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}
, so the commutativity of (
4.2
) is possible only if the first components of
f^{\prime}
and
h
coincide. Since
(f^{\prime}_{1}\otimes f^{\prime}_{1})S\subset I_{2}
, the formula
h_{1}=f^{\prime}_{1}
correctly defines the graded homomorphism
h\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to TV/(I_{2})
and the diagram (
4.2
) is commutative. Thus for any
f\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
there is a unique
h\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to G{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\cdot h=f
. By virtue of Theorem
2.13
we get a right adjoint
G\colon{\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}
for the embedding
{\mathbf{QA}}\hookrightarrow{\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}}
such that
h=Gf
. By taking the first graded components in the commutative diagrams (
4.2
), (
4.2
) we see that the first component
(Gf)_{1}\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\to V
coincides with
f_{1}\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{1}=V
. Since
{\mathcal{B}}
is generated by
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
, this formula uniquely defines the whole graded homomorphism
Gf\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to G{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(I_{2})
.

Now let us prove that
\phi_{{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}=G(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\circ\varepsilon_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}})\colon G{\mathcal{A}}\circ G{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\to G({\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
is an isomorphism for arbitrary
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebras
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\in{\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}}
. Let
V={\mathcal{A}}_{1}
,
V^{\prime}={\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{1}
, the morphism
\phi_{{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
is uniquely defined by its first order component
(\phi_{{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}})_{1}=\big(G(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\circ\varepsilon_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}})\big)_{1}=(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\circ\varepsilon_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}})_{1}=(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})_{1}\otimes(\varepsilon_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}})_{1}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime}}
. Denote by
I=\bigoplus\limits_{k\geqslant 2}I_{k}
,
I^{\prime}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\geqslant 2}I^{\prime}_{k}
the kernels of
a\colon TV\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}
and
a^{\prime}\colon TV^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
respectively, where
I_{k}=I\cap V^{\otimes k}
,
I^{\prime}_{k}=I^{\prime}\cap(V^{\prime})^{\otimes k}
. The kernel of the composition
T(V\otimes V^{\prime})\cong TV\circ TV^{\prime}\xrightarrow{a\circ a^{\prime}}G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}\circ G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is a graded ideal
J=\bigoplus\limits_{k\geqslant 2}J_{k}
with components
J_{k}=J\cap(V\otimes V^{\prime})^{\otimes k}
. The subalgebra
G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}\circ G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
coincides with
G^{\prime}({\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
, hence
G({\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})=T(V\otimes V^{\prime})/(J_{2})
.
By taking into account the isomorphisms
G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}\cong TV/I
and
G^{\prime}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\cong TV^{\prime}/I^{\prime}
we see that the ideal
J
is the kernel of the composition

\displaystyle T(V\otimes V^{\prime})\cong TV\circ TV^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow TV/(I_{2})\circ TV^{\prime}/(I^{\prime}_{2})\twoheadrightarrow TV/I\circ TV^{\prime}/I^{\prime},

(4.24)

where the epimorphisms
\twoheadrightarrow
are the Manin products of the corresponding projections. The second order component of the right epimorphism in (
4.24
) is the identity map, so the kernel of the second order component of

\displaystyle T(V\otimes V^{\prime})\cong TV\circ TV^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow TV/(I_{2})\circ TV^{\prime}/(I^{\prime}_{2})

(4.25)

equals
J_{2}
. Since (
4.25
) is a morphism of quadratic algebras, its kernel is the ideal
(J_{2})
. Hence we obtain the isomorphism
TV/(I_{2})\circ TV^{\prime}/(I^{\prime}_{2})\cong T(V\otimes V^{\prime})/(J_{2})
, whose first order component is the identity map
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V^{\prime}}
. This isomorphism is exactly the morphism
\phi_{{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}\colon G{\mathcal{A}}\circ G{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\to G({\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
. In particular,
\phi_{{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism for any
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
, so the conditions of Theorem
2.26
are fulfilled. ∎

Since
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})\in{\mathbf{FQA}}\subset{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
for any
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
, Prop.
2.20
implies that the category
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
is also coclosed relative to
{\mathbf{FQA}}
. The internal cohom-functor in
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
or
({\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}},\circ)
with parametrising subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}
equals to the composition of the functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{FQA}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}
and the embedding
{\mathbf{FQA}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
or
{\mathbf{FQA}}\hookrightarrow{\text{${\mathbb{Z}}$-$\mathbf{GrAlg}$}}
.

Conclusion

General representation theory.

Relative adjunctions introduced by Ulmer in
[
Ulm
]
can be used to generalise the notion of closed monoidal category in such way that the approach of
[
S21
, § 3]
works literally for this more general case. Thus we obtain a general representation theory for relatively closed monoidal categories and for monoidal functors between such categories. In particular, we described the tensor product of representations and how it is translated by a monoidal functor. Any representation can be considered as a left action, however we proved all the statements about representations independently from the theory of actions, we used the natural transformations
\pi
and
\Phi
.

Classical representations and semi-linear spaces.

This general approach covers the classical types of representations on vector spaces: representations of algebras, groups, monoids, Lie groups and algebraic groups. To interpret representations of groups and monoids as representations in the general sense we introduced semi-linear spaces with some monoidal product. The monoids in the monoidal category of semi-linear spaces (the semi-linear monoids) generalise algebras, monoids and groups, so the representation theory of such monoids includes the classical representations. The same can be done for the groups and monoids with geometric structures.

Quantum Representation Theory.

We proved that the categories of the
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
-graded and, more generally, of the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebras with the Manin product ‘
\circ
’ are relatively closed, where the parametrising subcategory consists of the connected finitely generated quadratic algebras:
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}
. This means that the general representation theory described here is applicable for quantum representations introduced in
[
S21
, § 5.1]
. We expect that it will be also applied to generalise quantum representations for other cases such as super-case, topological case, corepresentations on the connected algebras, a quantum version of the projective representations.
